# PREFACE

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'I' depicting a figure sitting in a chariot pulled by two large birds through the clouds, set against a background of sunbeams.</image-desc>The **Treatise on Pneumatics** <note>original: "Trattato delli Spirituali." In this context, "Spirituals" refers to the study of air, breath, and gases—what we now call pneumatics.</note> was judged by ancient **Philosophers** and **Mechanics** to be worthy of the greatest study. This was true particularly for those who treated the theory and power of this faculty, and also for those who considered its observable effects. Therefore, we have judged it principally necessary—wishing to treat this faculty ourselves—to gather in an orderly manner everything that was left on this subject by those Ancients, and also to explain in the best possible manner what we ourselves have discovered. We do this so that those who wish to apply themselves to the **Mathematical sciences** <note>In the 17th century, "Mathematics" encompassed a broad range of physical sciences, including engineering and mechanics.</note> might be aided by them as much as possible. Beyond this, considering this Treatise to be consistent with what we already described in four books regarding **Water-Clocks** <note>original: "Horoscopij Acquatici." These are clepsydrae or devices that measure time using the flow of water.</note>, we have decided to write it. For through the combination of air, fire, water, and earth—primarily of three **Elements**, or perhaps even all four—and through their mixing together, various arrangements are produced. Some of these are very necessary for human use and life, while others bring us a certain admiration full of unspeakable wonder. But before we enter into what we intend to say, it is necessary for us to discuss the **vacuum** <note>The nature of the vacuum—whether empty space could exist in nature—was one of the most significant debates in ancient and early modern physics.</note>.

-><image-desc>Woodcut decorative frieze featuring repeating floral and foliate motifs.</image-desc><-

<summary>This preface introduces the study of pneumatics, framing it as a vital discipline inherited from ancient engineers and philosophers that combines the four elements to create both practical tools and wondrous machines.</summary>

<keywords>pneumatics, Hero of Alexandria, vacuum, four elements, water-clocks, mechanics, ancient physics</keywords>

# ON THE VACUUM IN THE BOOK
# OF PNEUMATICS
## For the understanding of the Work.

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'M' containing the letter 'M' surrounded by ornate floral and foliage patterns.</image-desc> *Many have universally said, or rather affirmed, that there is no such thing as empty space; others thought that by nature no accumulated vacuum <note>original: "uacuo coaceruato," referring to a large, continuous empty space</note> exists, but rather that it exists by means of certain small parts scattered within the air, the water, the fire, and in other bodies; and to these it is necessary to agree. But regarding all that which falls under the senses, and which appears manifest in the following pages, we will strive to show that it is so and not otherwise. As an example of this, we say that to many who do not consider further, Vessels appear empty; but they are not empty as they think, no, but filled with air. And air, as is pleasing to the natural philosophers <note>original: "naturali," those who study the laws of nature and physics</note>, is composed of small and light bodies, for the most part neither understood nor seen by us. For if into the vessel which, as we have said, appears empty to us, someone pours in water, as much water as enters the vessel, just as much air will exit from it; wherefore from this, everyone will be able to understand what we have said above. And they may understand also that if someone, having taken the vessel (which, as we say, will seem empty to us), submerges it upside down into the water, always holding it straight, there is no doubt that the water will not enter into it, even if it is forcibly driven entirely under the water. Whence it becomes clear to us that, air being a body, it will not allow the water to enter, because all the space in the vessel is filled with air; and this will be seen by pulling it straight out of the water. For by setting the interior surface of it upright, it will be found to be dry and clean as it was before it was submerged in the water. But if, as has been said, while the vessel is upside down and straight in the water, one drills a hole in the bottom, the water will enter through the mouth of it, and the air will exit through the said hole. Whence we must judge that air is a body which, when moved, becomes spirit <note>original: "spirito," which in this context refers to "pneuma" or air in motion/breath</note>, since spirit is nothing other than moved air; and if, having a hole in the bottom and being submerged in the water, someone places their hand over the hole, without doubt they will feel the spirit that exits from that vessel, and this is nothing else but air driven out by the water. Nor should we judge that in these things which are empty a certain accumulated nature persists, but rather that it exists according to some small parts scattered within the air, the water, and in other bodies—unless perchance there is someone who believes that the diamond <note>original: "d:amante" (diamante), the hardest known material</note> alone is entirely devoid of any vacuum, since it can neither be burned nor broken, but rather, placed upon the anvils and struck with very heavy hammers, it enters entirely into those anvils and hammers. Nor should this be attributed to it because, by its solid nature, it is devoid of vacuum, but rather because of the continuous density that is in it; for the small bodies of fire being larger than the vacuum, which*

<summary>This page introduces the fundamental pneumatic theory that air is a physical body that occupies space, arguing against a large "collected" vacuum while supporting the existence of microscopic voids between particles. It illustrates these concepts through experiments with inverted vessels submerged in water and the extreme density of diamonds.</summary>

<keywords>vacuum, pneumatics, Hero of Alexandria, air pressure, displacement, atomic theory, diamond, physics experiments</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced the idea that air is a substance composed of tiny particles and that what looks like an empty vessel is actually full of air. This page continues the argument, explaining how air can be compressed and how a vacuum behaves in nature. The catchword "do," from the previous page leads into the starting word "è" (is).</meta>

-># ON THE VACUUM<-

<meta>Header: ON THE VACUUM</meta>

*...is in the stone; they do not enter into the body of it, but stop at the exterior surface. For this reason, since they do not penetrate inside it, they do not introduce heat as happens in other bodies. But the bodies of the air have among them a certain coherence <term>coherence: the way particles stick together</term>, though not in every part; instead, it is through certain intervening intervals which we shall call vacuums <note>original: "vacui"</note>, like in the sand that is found on beaches. This can be understood by the mind: that the tiny particles of the sand are similar to the Aerial bodies, and that the air interspersed between the particles of the sand is similar to the vacuums contained within the air. When this air is forced by a violent power, it must condense as it enters those empty places. Once these bodies are forced and compressed, their nature is violated; but when the force that pressured it is let go and released, it must return again to its order through the natural struggle <note>original: "contentione," referring to the internal tension or pressure of a substance to return to its shape</note> that exists between natural bodies. This occurs just as it does with shavings of horn or with dry sponges: which, when compressed and then released, return to their former place and take up the same volume once more. The same occurs if the tiny particles in the air are pulled apart by a violent force, making the empty space larger than is natural; they quickly rush back into themselves. Because of the sudden evacuation, it is necessary for the bodies to return into themselves and to one another, regardless of anything that might oppose them. This can be seen if someone takes a very light vessel with a narrow mouth and, having drawn out the breath or the air inside it with their mouth, immediately lets go; the said vessel will hang from that person's lips, and the vacuum will draw in the flesh, as its nature is being forced, until the empty space is refilled. This most clearly demonstrates to us that the space inside the body of the vessel had been totally empty. This is also manifest from another reason. Those vessels that you doctors call "cupping glasses" <note>original: "vasi, che voui Medici si chiamano," referring to medical ventose used for bloodletting or therapy</note>, which are made of glass with a very small mouth: when one wants to fill them with water, they suck through the mouth to draw out the air, then immediately submerge them in water. Once the finger is removed from the mouth, the water is drawn upward by the vacuum; thus one sees the empty space filled, and that water, forced by the power of the vacuum, is carried upward against its own nature. What is clear from these examples is certainly not foreign to what we discussed above, it being most certain that once a body is removed, not only is its manifest weight not released, but the underlying matter is drawn in by the same cause due to the rarity of the body. Furthermore, when fire is placed in these bodies, it corrupts and thins the air contained within them, just as other bodies are corrupted by these fires and transformed into thinner substances—I mean air, water, and earth. That they are corrupted by fire is manifest from burnt coals, which keep the same volume they had before combustion (or slightly less), yet are of much lesser weight. Those substances that are corrupted in the bodies pass through smoke into fiery, aerial, and earthy substance; for the thinner parts are carried, being lighter, into the higher place where the fire is above the air and below the circle of the Moon <note>In the ancient and medieval worldview, the "sublunary" sphere was the region of change and the four elements below the Moon</note>. Those that are a little thicker remain in the air, and the heaviest parts rise with them for a short time, but being unable to stay there because of their continuous weight, they descend again to the lower part and are added to the earth. Water, too, corrupted by fire, is changed into air; for the vapors that rise from boiling vessels are nothing other than the thinning of moisture.*

<vocab>vacuum, air, particles, fire, Doctors, weight (gravity), vapors, heat, coherence</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the "interstitial vacuum" theory, comparing air particles to grains of sand and describing how compression and suction function. It provides experimental evidence for vacuums using cupping glasses and explains how fire transforms elements into lighter substances like smoke and vapor.</summary>
<keywords>Hero of Alexandria, vacuum, pneumatics, cupping glasses, compression, elemental transformation, 17th-century physics, Aristotelian cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical properties of air and the transformation of elements. The previous page ended by describing how air particles rush back together after being pulled apart, creating a vacuum. The fragment "do." at the start of this page completes the word "secondo" (according) or a similar construction from the previous page's final sentence.</meta>

-># ON THE VACUUM<-

<center>ON THE VACUUM,</center>

**A 2**
<meta>catchword: respi.</meta>

...that pass into the air: so it is manifest that fire dissolves and transforms everything thicker than itself, and that from the exhalations <term>exhalation: vapors or fumes thought to be released by the earth when heated</term> produced by the earth, the thickest bodies are transformed into thinner substances. In no other way does dew rise high unless the water on the earth is thinned by its exhalation, and that exhalation is produced by a certain fiery substance of the Sun that is within the bowels of the earth, which heats that place. This occurs even more if the earth is sulfurous or bituminous, as such heated material usually generates exhalations; and the waters found in the earth become hot for the same reasons. The thinner part of the dew is then transformed into air, while its thicker part, forced by the power of the exhalation, rises somewhat high; then, cooled by the turning away of the Sun, it falls back down upon the earth.

But winds are born from the powerful exhalation of the air, being thinned and driven out by its continuous motion. The motion of the air is not equally fast; rather, it is much faster at the beginning, near the exhalation, and always becomes slower and weaker the further it moves from the place it started. This also happens with heavy objects being carried upward: for their motion is much faster near the place where the violence <note>In early physics, "violence" refers to an external force acting against an object's natural tendency</note> that drives them is applied, and slower in the higher part. Because they are not accompanied by the same driving force that originally moved them, they return again to their natural place from whence they departed—that is, to the lower parts. For if they were always accompanied by that same driving force at an equal speed, they would surely never stop; but as the force gradually ceases, one sees the speed of the moved object cease as well.

Water also transforms into an earthy substance; for when we dig into the ground and pour water into a hollow place, it vanishes shortly after, having been absorbed by the earthy substance and, by mixing with it, becomes earth. If there is anyone who says that the water is merely constricted <note>original: "si costringe," meaning condensed or compressed</note> and not absorbed by the earth, but rather evaporates and dries up due to the heat of the Sun or some other cause, he is truly in error. For if the same water is poured into a vessel of glass, copper, or another dense material and exposed to the Sun for a great span of time, only a tiny part of it will diminish. Thus, it is clear that water transforms into an earthy substance, creating what we might call the stickiness or mucilage of the earth.

Just as water transforms into an earthy substance, a thin substance can also change into a thicker one. We see this in extinguished lamps that have run out of oil: the flame is carried somewhat upward and, as if driven out, departs from its own place toward its supreme place above the air. However, being overcome by the many intervening layers of air, it is not carried to its destined place; instead, mixed and entangled by the aerial bodies, it turns into air. 

The same must be understood of the air itself: if we submerge a small vessel into water and then uncover it so that the water enters through the mouth above, the air will certainly depart from the vessel; otherwise, being overcome by the great quantity of water, it will be mixed and entangled again in such a way that it becomes water. In the same way, the air corrupted within cupping glasses <term>cupping glasses: medical instruments (cucurbitule or ventose) heated and placed on the skin to create a vacuum</term> and thinned by fire, exits through the porousness <note>original: "rarità," referring to the microscopic gaps in the material</note> of the vessel. Once the body is rendered a vacuum, it draws toward itself the surrounding matter, whatever its quality may be. But when the cupping glass...

<vocab>exhalation, dew, air, fire, Sun, transmutation, water, earth, cupping glasses, suction, vacuum</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the vacuum, specifically addressing the debate between theoretical philosophers and empirical practitioners. It follows a discussion on how air rushes to fill empty spaces and transitions here into a mechanical experiment involving a metal sphere to prove that air can be compressed into minute, "disseminated" empty spaces between particles.</meta>

# ON THE VACUUM.

*...it will breathe, air succeeding into the evacuated space, it will no longer pull the matter: and if universally anyone should say that nothing at all is a vacuum, many arguments could be found to demonstrate this, and one could be persuaded by forces of words, given that they bring no sensible demonstration; but in those things which appear clearly and which fall under the senses, if they show the vacuum to be certainly coacervated <term>coacervated: gathered or heaped together into a single mass, as opposed to tiny gaps between particles</term>, and made outside of its Nature, and to be disseminated in small parts, and those bodies fill the disseminated Vacuums through compression, one certainly should not lend an ear to those who labor to bring probable reasons for this. For, having constructed a sphere, the thickness of which is of a metal plate so that it cannot easily be broken: but well-made, and excellently sealed all around; then having pierced it, and in the hole placed a copper pipe, which does not strike the pierced place opposite according to the diameter <note>The tube must not touch the far side of the sphere, allowing air to circulate freely within the vessel</note>; so that the water can flow; and making the other part of the pipe extend out of the sphere about three fingers' length, and let the perimeter of the hole through which the pipe is placed be sealed with tin <note>original: "stagno"; likely referring to solder</note>, so that then if we close that pipe and the outer surface of the sphere, so that when we wish to blow into it with our mouth, the spirit <term>spirit: here meaning air or breath, from the Latin "spiritus"</term> can in no way escape.*

*We will see what is contained within it, which is certainly nothing other than the air existing in it, in the same way that happens in those other vessels called empty, all of which are full, and by a certain continuation applied to their perimeter, finally no sort of vacuum being able to be there, no water nor other air can be placed therein; that which was inside before not leaving; rather it will happen that if we use violence <note>In early physics, "violence" refers to an external force acting against an object's natural tendency</note> to place some in, the vessel will break before it can receive any at all, because it is full, for not even the bodies of air can be contracted into smaller sizes; because it would be necessary for certain intervals to be made between them, in which the compressed bodies were of smaller bulk. Which is not possible, there being no vacuum at all: and when according to all surfaces the bodies were applied together, similarly in the perimeter of the vessel, being forced, they cannot give place to other bodies, there being no vacuum; and for this reason, in no way can any of those bodies that are outside be put into the proposed sphere, if first some part of the air previously contained in it does not leave. If, however, the whole place is packed and continuous, as is thought.*

*But if someone comes to blow into the sphere through the mouth of the pipe, they will certainly introduce much spirit, without the air that is in it leaving; which, as it is always thus, manifestly demonstrates that in the sphere a contraction occurs of those bodies that are involved in the vacuums. But in this, the contraction occurs because Nature is violated by the violent admission of the spirit: if therefore, blowing through that mouth, we place our hand there and immediately block the hole with a finger, the packed air will always remain in the sphere: But if we open that mouth, the air let in will burst out again and flee with a very great noise and screeching <note>original: "strepito, e cridore"; describing the high-pitched sound of pressurized air escaping a small orifice</note>. For as has been proposed, it is driven out by the expansion of the pre-existing air, made with a certain impetus: Again, if someone wishes to draw out the air that is in the proposed sphere with their mouth through the pipe, a very great quantity will be pulled out, and yet no other substance will succeed into the sphere, as was said above regarding the Medical Egg <note>original: "Ouo Medico"; a reference to a cupping glass used in ancient medicine to create suction on the skin</note>. Whereby it is clearly demonstrated that in the vacuum of the sphere a very great coacervation had occurred; for...*

# ON THE VACUUM.

because the bodies of air, which are left there at the same time, cannot become
larger: so much so that they fill the place of the expelled bodies: for if they increased
without adding other external substance, it would be likely that this increase would
happen through rarefaction: but this is an implication by way of evacuation, and because
no vacuum is granted, they cannot even increase the bodies, since not even with the mind

within the bodies of air there are disseminated certain vacuums, which, overtaken by a certain violence <note>In this context, "violence" refers to an external physical force or pressure applied against the natural state of the element.</note>,
are forced against nature to retreat into vacuums; whence the air that is enclosed in a vessel
submerged in water, even if it comes to be much pressed: however, that which by right should
force it is not sufficient in this place, because naturally water in itself
has neither gravity nor vehement compression: as we see happen to those
who urinate in the depths of the Sea, who, even if they have above their shoulders infinite *metretes* <term>metretes: an ancient Greek liquid measure, roughly 9 gallons or 34 liters</term>,
or Amphorae <term>Amphore: large ceramic jars used by the ancients for transporting liquids like wine or oil</term>, are not forced by the water to

But how it happens that those who swim in the Sea are not compressed by the infinite weight
of the water they have above their shoulders and above their waist is certainly worthy
of consideration. Some say this happens because the water is equally heavy according to
itself; but these do not say for what reason those who swim in the depths are not
compressed by the upper water, which certainly must be demonstrated in this way.
Let it be understood that there is a certain body equally heavy and equally humid, having the
same form or figure as the upper liquid, of which the upper surface is like that of the
compressed thing; and let us understand this as being thrown by us into the water, and let it be
that the lower surface of it matches the upper one, or rather is just like it, and similarly let it
be placed equal to the upper liquid; it is very clear that this body submerged in the water will
not stay afloat above it, nor will it sink beneath the surface of the upper liquid, which
is learnedly demonstrated by Archimedes in the book on those bodies of equal weight <note>original: "corpi egualmente graui"; a reference to Archimedes' treatise *On Floating Bodies*</note>,
in which he also proves that a liquid immersed in a liquid neither floats upon it nor
sinks into it. It is seen, then, that bodies placed under the water cannot be compressed
by its gravity. For it can be said: how can that body be compressed which is not permitted
to descend into a lower place? And for this reason, the liquid where the body was
cannot compress the bodies placed beneath it. Because as for the extreme, which belongs
to the reasons of motion and of rest, there is no difference at all between the said body
and the liquid that occupies the same place; but if anyone understands there to be no
vacuum—it not being granted and not existing—then light, or heat, or any other bodily
power could not pass through water, nor through air, nor through any other body.
For how would the rays of the Sun pass through the water to the bottom of the vessel if
the water had no porosity? There is no doubt those rays would break the water with
violence, which would cause the full vessels to overflow. Which we do not see happen;
and for this reason, if they broke the water with their violence, certainly some of them
would be broken in the upper part; some others would fall downwards; nor are they
seen, striking the particles of the water, to be broken in the upper place. But rather,
falling into the water and passing through small particles,

<meta>This page concludes the introductory theoretical discussion on the nature of the vacuum. It transitions from the proof of "disseminated" vacuums (tiny spaces between particles) to the rejection of a "coacervated" vacuum (a large, continuous empty space) in nature. It sets the stage for the practical mechanical theorems that follow.</meta>

<header>ON THE VACUUM.</header>

AG-

...particles, they go to the bottom of the vessel: which clearly makes us understand that in water there are vacuums. Furthermore, one sees wine poured into water, according to its pouring out, moving through it: which would not happen if there were no vacuums in the water; and lights are carried through one another; for if we light more lamps, they will illuminate everything more in the same way, passing and penetrating through one another interchangeably. But even through copper, and through iron, and through all other bodies, such penetration occurs in exactly the same way as happens in the *torpedo* fish <term>torpedine: the electric ray; ancient and Renaissance scientists believed its numbing power was a physical force that could penetrate solid objects like spears or nets</term>. But because we have demonstrated that a large accumulated vacuum <note>original: "vacuo amassato"</note> is against nature—whether by the experiment of the light vessel held against the mouth, or by the *medical egg* <term>Ouo medico: a "cupping glass," a vessel used by physicians to create a partial vacuum and draw blood or fluids to the skin's surface</term>—and as it seems to us that the many demonstrations of the nature of the vacuum explained by us are sufficient, we have thought we have said enough of this, since we have shown it through sensible demonstrations.

Let it therefore be universally permitted for us to say that every body is composed of light and small bodies, in which, or between which, are small vacuums disseminated in particles; and that we misuse terms when we say "nothing is found to be empty" unless it is forced by some violence <note>In this context, "violence" refers to an external physical force that overcomes an object's natural state.</note>; but rather that every thing is full, either of air, or of water, or of some other substance, and as much as one of these is missing, so much of the other there is, which fills the place. We say again that no vacuum is naturally coacervated <term>coacervato: gathered into a heap or a large, continuous empty space, as opposed to tiny gaps between atoms</term>, or accumulated, unless it is forced by some violence, and again that no vacuum is found totally except against nature. And since we have explained these things, it is now time to begin describing the Theorems, which are produced by the interactions <note>original: "battaglie" (battles or struggles), describing the dynamic pressure and competition for space between elements</note> of the aforementioned Elements; for by means of these are found various and marvelous motions. Having first considered these as Elements, we shall now reason about bent siphons, as they are very useful for many pneumatic <term>Spirituali: a term derived from the Greek "pneumatikos," referring to machines or devices operated by air, steam, or water pressure</term> matters.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the nature of the vacuum. While the previous page focused on Hero of Alexandria's theories regarding tiny, disseminated vacuums within matter, this "Addition" by the editor/translator Gian Battista Aleotti provides practical, contemporary experiments involving firearms to further illustrate these physical principles.</meta>

# ADDITION
## BY ALEOTTI
### Concerning the impossibility of any vacuum, and how the Element of Air cannot remain compressed.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial "I" featuring acanthus leaf motifs and scrolls.</image-desc>
In conformity with what Hero has said above, one can add the following: if we take a musket ramrod <note>original: "bachetta d’ Arcobugio"</note> topped with a well-made scraper <term>rascatore: a tool used to clean the interior of a firearm barrel, here serving as a piston</term>, and we thrust it into the barrel of an arquebus <term>Arcobugio: an early muzzle-loaded firearm; the predecessor to the musket</term> that has been perfectly and straightly bored with the highest excellence, and then close its touch-hole <term>fogone: the small hole in a firearm where the powder is ignited</term>; if we then pull the rod almost all the way out—which will be done with some difficulty, as the vacuum remaining in the lower part resists us because air cannot enter to fill it—and if, having pulled it nearly out, I say, we release it, that vacuum (because it cannot exist except through violated nature) will violently pull the said rod back in order to fill itself instantly. 

Likewise, as proof that the Element of Air cannot remain in any state other than its natural quality as God Almighty created it: if, with the touch-hole of that same barrel closed, we thrust the aforementioned ramrod into it, we will feel (because Air is a body) that we do so with effort, and that the Air will become packed together <note>original: "amassarsi"</note>. And if, having pushed it down as far as we can, we release it freely, the violated air, being unable to remain compressed <note>original: "constipato"</note>, will break forth and with fury drive out the rod in order to return immediately to its natural state once the violence has ceased. From this it becomes clear to us that by thrusting a ball into it while the touch-hole remains closed, the compressed air, seeking to return to its nature, drives it out with violence. And if the first experiment demonstrates that a vacuum cannot exist, the second makes it clear to us that this Element cannot remain except within the limits of its nature, just as its Creator created it.

It is further proved that no vacuum can exist by those glass vessels that women are accustomed to using to diminish and in part evacuate...

<summary>Gian Battista Aleotti provides practical experiments using a musket barrel to demonstrate that nature resists the creation of a vacuum and that compressed air exerts force to return to its natural volume.</summary>
<keywords>Gian Battista Aleotti, vacuum, air pressure, compression, arquebus, physics experiments, pneumatics, atmospheric pressure</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Addition" by the editor Gian Battista Aleotti to Hero of Alexandria’s *Pneumatics*. While the previous page used the mechanical example of a firearm's ramrod to demonstrate the resistance of a vacuum, this page moves into medical and industrial observations—specifically breast pumps and kilns—to further illustrate the principle that nature abhors a vacuum.</meta>

...draining milk from the breasts, which after childbirth, within a period of two or three days, usually arrives in such abundance that if not emptied for the newborn infants, it would cause (if the breasts are not relieved) hardness and very grave ailments. These [devices], as is well known, have a body with a hole so large that by placing the vessel against the breast, the nipple <term>capitello: literally "little head," referring here to the nipple or teat</term> enters comfortably inside. In another part, they have a neck so long that they take it into their mouth, and once the air inside the vessel is sucked out, the milk immediately follows in its place, flowing out of the breast. 

And regarding those flasks that they also typically use for this same purpose: they take a glass flask <note>original: "ampolla di vetro"</note> with a neck wide enough at the top to accommodate the nipple, and they heat the body of it thoroughly with fire until the heat, penetrating through the pores <term>vacui: referring here to the microscopic spaces or pores in the glass</term> of the thin glass, drives out the air, filling the body of the flask with a very subtle vapor. When said body is well heated, they immediately place the mouth of the flask's neck against the breast, inserting the nipple. Because that subtle fiery vapor <note>original: "vapore igneo"</note> cannot remain enclosed there, it escapes through those same pores in the glass through which it entered, rising toward its natural place. Even if it is transformed into an aerial substance by the surrounding air, since air cannot enter through these extremely fine passages <term>meati: channels or pores</term> and there can be no vacuum, that body—which cannot remain empty—immediately draws the milk from the breast. By emptying the breast, it fills itself; and once completely full, it draws no more, just as it would if it were left open in some part to let the air enter.

Similarly, the fires that are lit at the mouths of furnaces (in which stones, lime, and earthenware vessels are fired) are drawn inside those furnaces by the vacuum. For the vapor of the fire, having driven out the air inside, vanishes and evaporates upward. Since the fire is at the mouth of the furnace, it prevents the air from entering. But because there cannot be a vacuum as the vapor vanishes, the fire must fill the empty space that would remain in the furnace; because as the vapor exits, the path for the air is closed, and since there can be no vacuum, it is necessary for the fire to follow. From these things, it is evident with what excellence Hero <note>Hero of Alexandria, the 1st-century engineer whose work Aleotti is expanding upon.</note> has proven that a total vacuum is not permitted unless forced and contrary to nature.

<summary>This page describes practical applications of vacuum theory, explaining how breast pumps use suction or heat-displaced air to draw milk, and how furnaces draw in fire to fill empty space. It concludes by affirming Hero of Alexandria's belief that nature naturally resists the formation of a vacuum.</summary>

<keywords>vacuum theory, breast pumps, glass flasks, furnace dynamics, Hero of Alexandria, Gian Battista Aleotti, atmospheric pressure, fiery vapor</keywords>

# ON PNEUMATICS
# BY HERO,
## Translated by Master Giovanni Battista Aleotti
## OF ARGENTA.

---

->*ON DRAWING WATER BY MEANS OF A*<-
->*BENT TUBE, OR PIPE. Theorem One.*<-

Let there be water in a vessel A. B., the surface of which is F. G. Within this vessel, let there be a bent pipe C.D.E. <note>Hero refers to a siphon</note> inserted with one leg, and let the leg C. H. be in the water, which will necessarily be filled with water up to H., level with the surface F. G., while the part H.D.I. remains full of air. I say that if we make a hole at I., and through it we draw out the aforementioned air with our mouth, the moisture—that is, the water—will follow it. This is because, as stated above, it is clear that a place cannot be entirely empty <note>original: "vacuo"; a reference to the classical belief that nature abhors a vacuum</note>. To this it must be added that if the hole I. through which we have drawn the air is level with the surface F.G., the pipe will not spill, but the water will remain at that limit in such a way that the part C.D.I. will remain full of it, even though, contrary to the order of nature, it remains suspended on high like a balanced scale, with the water being elevated from H. to D. and suspended downward from D. to I.

<image-desc>A woodcut diagram illustrating a siphon experiment. An ornate classical-style urn, labeled A and B, contains liquid with a surface level marked by a horizontal line F-G. A bent tube (siphon) marked C.D.E. is inserted into the urn. Point H is the bottom of the tube inside the liquid. Point D is the highest bend of the tube. Point I is an aperture on the outer vertical leg, level with the inner surface F-G. Point K is a lower aperture on the same leg, and E is the bottom of the tube. The urn is decorated with two dragon-shaped handles.</image-desc>

But if the hole at the end of the pipe is in a straight line as at K. <note>meaning, at a lower point than the water's surface</note>, the pipe will spill, and the water will flow out; because the part D.K., being heavier than the part D.H. <note>D.K. is the longer, heavier column of water</note>, will prevail and pull the latter, and it will flow out of this channel until the surface of the water (which, as the channel flows, will continuously drop in the vessel) has reached the level of the hole K.; and at that point it will no longer flow—

<meta>catchword: do (likely leading to "donde" or "dopo")</meta>

<summary>Hero of Alexandria describes the fundamental mechanics of a siphon, explaining how air suction creates a vacuum that draws water up a tube, and how gravity allows it to flow if the exit is lower than the source.</summary>
<keywords>Hero of Alexandria, Aleotti, siphon, pneumatics, hydraulics, vacuum, atmospheric pressure, water displacement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Theorem One from Hero of Alexandria’s *Pneumatics*, as interpreted by Aleotti. The previous page introduced the basic setup of a siphon; here, Aleotti investigates the physical causes behind why water flows through a bent tube, refuting common misconceptions about the "weight" of the water pulling itself along.</meta>

# ON PNEUMATICS

...will stop for the same aforementioned reason; but if we make the hole at E., this water will flow out until the water in the vessel has dropped so far that its surface is level with the mouth of the pipe C. And if we wish to draw all the water out of the vessel, we shall lower the mouth C. to the very bottom of the vessel, though as far from it as we think will suffice for the water to flow. Some say the reason why the pierced and bent <term>pipe<note>original: "canna"; used here to describe the siphon tube</note></term> produces this effect is because the quantity of water in the longer <term>leg<note>original: "gamba"; referring to the vertical sections of the siphon</note></term> has the power to attract, and in effect pulls, the smaller [leg]. But how false this cause is, and in what error whoever believes this remains, may be seen from this: Let a pipe be made such that the interior leg is long and thin, and the exterior leg much shorter but wider, so that it holds a greater quantity of water than the long leg. If it were filled with water, and the longer leg then placed in a vessel of water or a well—which would be the same as if we made the exterior leg flow—since it has in itself a greater abundance of water than the interior one, it would also have the power to attract the water from the larger [leg], and with it would also pull that which is in the well. When it began to flow, it would draw it all out or flow forever, because the quantity of the exterior water is greater than that in the interior leg. But since the truth of where this derives from does not appear, we do not approve of the aforementioned reason. 

Instead, let us look at the natural cause of this, saying that every <term>continuous fluid<note>original: "humido continuo"; a Renaissance term for any liquid that maintains a physical connection without gaps</note></term> and still liquid takes a **spherical surface** whose center is the same as the Earth’s. But if it is not standing still, it flows until it reduces itself to a spherical surface, as was said above. Let two vessels be taken by us, and in each of them let water be placed; let us also fill the pipe with water, and with our fingers let us plug its mouths, placing one end in one of the aforesaid vessels so that it is submerged in the water, and similarly let us place the other leg in the other vessel. All the water is thus made continuous, because the water in both vessels becomes joined with that in the pipe in such a way that it is all one continuous body. If, therefore, the said waters which were first in the vessels are at the same surface level, being made continuous by the bent pipe submerged in them, they will rest and remain still. But if one of them is lower than the other, because the water is made continuous, it is also necessary by this continuity that the higher one flows into the lower one, until either all the water in the aforesaid vessels is reduced to the same surface level, or until one of the said vessels is empty. But if they equalize at the same surface level, the waters in these vessels will stop, both one and the other, so that the water in the pipe will also remain still. Thus, given that one leg and the other are equally submerged in each of said surfaces (supposing they are equal), the water within it will stay still. If the pipe is suspended so that it declines neither here nor there, it is again necessary that the water stops, whether it has equal width or if one leg is much larger than the other; for in this, the reason why the water stands still or flows does not depend on that, but derives from whether its mouths in the water are level. 

Now let us say: why (when the pipe is suspended) does the water not flow down by its own **gravity** <note>In this context, "gravity" refers to the natural heaviness or weight of the liquid</note>, it being lighter than the air beneath it? It is for no other reason, certainly, than because the place of the whole cannot be...

<meta>catchword: fer (likely for "fermare," to stop/stay)</meta>

<summary>Aleotti refutes the idea that siphons work because a larger volume of water "pulls" a smaller one, arguing instead that water flows to seek a uniform level relative to the Earth's center.</summary>
<keywords>siphon, hydraulics, fluid mechanics, vacuum, spherical surface, gravity, equilibrium, continuous fluid</keywords>

be a <term>vacuum<note>original: "vacuo"; refers to the lack of air that allows the siphon to function</note></term>: because, if the water must come out, it is necessary that the upper part of the pipe first fills, into which air cannot enter by any means. Wherefore, if we <term>pierce<note>original: "pertugiaremo"; to make a small hole or aperture</note></term> it in the upper part, the water will immediately come out, and in its place the air will succeed: but before said hole is made, the moisture—that is, the water that is in the pipe—strikes against the subject air, which, having no place where it can flow, does not let the water out: but when, by means of the hole, the air finds a place, then it gives way to the water and lets it flow out, filling its place. And for this reason, <term>against nature<note>meaning against the natural downward tendency of heavy elements like water</note></term>, one draws the wine through the pipe with the mouth: because by drawing the air that is in the pipe, one comes

to fill it much more, and by being joined to this air, we come to detach it. And this is done until, with the surface of the wine—as was said above—the evacuation is made, so that then the detached wine, flowing, falls into the evacuated place of the Tube, having no other place in which it is permitted to flow, and for this reason it is carried upwards against nature. Otherwise, the water in the pipe will come to rest when it is settled in a spherical surface, the center of which is the same as the center of the earth. For if there is any surface of water that has the same

<column-break/>

center as the earth, it remains still: but if it is possible that it does not remain still, it must be that by moving, it comes to rest. Let it be then, that the center of its spherical surface, being the same as that of the earth, follows the first surface: For water, flowing here and there through one and through many places, will have occupied different places; let it be, therefore, that each of those surfaces which have their center with the earth are cut by some plane, and by them lines are created in said surfaces, which are circles of the circumferences that have the same center as the earth, namely A.B.C.F. B.D. and let B.G. be drawn, which, because it will be equal to each of them—namely G.F. and G.A.—as is possible, it is therefore necessary that it comes to rest, and let so much be said of this.

---

## ON THE PNEUMATIC TUBE IN THE MIDDLE OF ANOTHER

->Tube closed at the top mouth. Theorem II.<-

There is another sort of pipe or Tube, which is called a middle <term>pneumatic<note>original: "Spiritale"; from the Greek "pneumatikos," relating to air, breath, or pressure</note></term> tube, for which the reasoning is the same as the past one of the bent pipe. Let the vessel full of water be A.B., in the middle of which is placed the Tube C.D., which, passing through the foot of said vessel, extends below it: but in the upper part, its mouth does not reach the mouth of the vessel A.B., but is surrounded by another Tube, the void of

# ON PNEUMATIC DEVICES

...which should be somewhat larger than the first tube, and equally distant from it; let the mouth E. F. of this one be stopped up most diligently, so that no air enters: but let the lower mouth G. H. of it be so distant from the bottom of the vessel that the water, wanting to exit from it, can flow freely. These being so arranged, as I have said,

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a decorative, footed urn or vessel, flanked by two seated lion-like figures on a common pedestal. The vessel is shown in cross-section to reveal a siphon mechanism composed of concentric internal tubes. Various parts are labeled with letters: A and B on the vessel's exterior; E and F at the top of an outer internal tube; C on an inner tube; G and H at the base of the tubes; and D at the top opening. Dotted horizontal lines represent fluid levels.</image-desc>

if we draw the air that is in the tube C. D. through the mouth D., we will consequently also draw the water that is in the vessel, all of which will come out because of that part of the tube that extends below the foot of the vessel. For the air that is between the water and the tube C. at I. K. in the tube E. F., being drawn through the mouth D., will pull the water with it; the flow of which will not stop because of the extension that is outside the vessel. But if the tube E. F. G. H. were not there, the flow of the water would cease, even if its surface were at C., the excess length remaining stationary. However, because air cannot enter the entire tube E. F. G. H. which is immersed in the water, the flow will not stop, and as the air that entered vessel A. B. exits, water will succeed in its place; because the mouth of the tube that is outside the vessel is always lower than the surface of the <term>liquid<note>original: "humido"; literally "the moist," referring to the water in the vessel</note></term> within it. And these surfaces being unable to become equal—due to the greater <term>weight<note>original: "grauità"; here referring to the hydrostatic pressure or weight of the water column</note></term> of the water—it will happen that all the water comes out of the vessel. And if we do not wish to draw out the air contained in the tubes C. D. and I. K. with our mouth, we shall fill the vessel A. B. with so much water that, by being poured through the tube C. D., it takes up the flow and discourse, and thus all the water in the vessel will exit. And this tube shall be called the <term>Pneumatic Siphon<note>original: "Siphone Spiritale"; 'spiritale' refers to the action of 'pneuma' or air/spirit within the mechanism</note></term>.

From what has been said, it is clear that the flow from the tube (while it remains stationary) will be unequal, and the same will happen if the water exits through a hole in the bottom of the vessel; for its flow will be unequal because at the beginning of the <term>effusion<note>original: "effusione"; the pouring out or discharge of fluid</note></term>, it is pressed by a greater weight, which, always becoming less as the water in the vessel drops, the flow becomes smaller and weaker. And the greater the excess length of the tube, the faster the flow becomes, and slower as it is smaller, as was also said in the previous proposition. It is manifest, then, from what we have said, that the flow of water through a tube or pipe is always unequal; therefore, proceeding further, we must demonstrate a flow of water that is always equal by means of the bent pipe proposed above.

# ON THE CONSTANT FLOW,
## By means of the bent tube. Theorem III.

Let there be a vessel A.B. filled with water up to the surface H.K., in which a bowl C.D. floats. The mouth of this bowl is to be very well sealed with a lid C.D., and in this lid, as well as in the bottom of the bowl, a hole is made through which one 

<image-desc>A woodcut diagram illustrating a hydraulic experiment. A large ornate vessel labeled A and B is filled with water to a level marked H and K. Floating inside is a smaller, lidded bowl labeled C and D. A siphon-like bent tube (E, F, G) passes through the lid and bottom of the floating bowl and exits the main vessel. The floating mechanism ensures that the head of the siphon remains at a constant depth relative to the water surface as it drops.</image-desc>

leg of the bent tube <term>E.F.G.</term> <note>This "bent tube" is a siphon. In early hydraulics, the flow rate of a siphon changes as the water level in the reservoir drops; Hero's invention solves this problem.</note> passes, as in the following example. These holes are to be excellently sealed with tin <note>original: "stagno"; referring to the use of tin solder to make the joints airtight</note> around the tube itself, assuming that we make the vessel of copper or a similar metal. The other leg of the tube is placed outside the vessel, its mouth being lower than the surface of the water in the vessel, as described above. If we draw the air out through the mouth of the tube that is outside the vessel using our own mouth, the water will likewise follow it; because there cannot be a completely empty space within the tube. Once the flow begins, it will continue until all the water in the vessel has exited. This flow will be equal <note>By "equal," Hero means a constant flow rate. Since the siphon is attached to a float, the vertical distance between the water surface and the siphon's intake remains the same even as the water level drops.</note>; because as the water surface falls, the bowl will also fall along with the tube fixed into it. And while the extent of the tube on the outside may be greater, making the flow of water faster, the flow itself will nonetheless remain constant.

# ON THE FLOW THROUGH THE BENT PIPE,
## Partly equal, and partly unequal. Theorem IV.

A flow that is sometimes equal and sometimes unequal can likewise be created by a bent pipe <note>original: "piegata canna"</note> according to our will; and at times, if we so desire, it can be made constant in itself, or faster, or slower than the first flow. Let there be, for example, the vessel A.B. full of water and the bowl C.D. covered as was said above. Through the middle of both the bottom and the lid, a tube is fixed that is wider than the interior leg of the bent pipe; in the following example, this is E.F. It is very well sealed with tin around the holes in the bottom and the lid of the bowl, assuming, as said before, that the vessel is made of copper. On each side of the vessel, two rules <note>original: "regoli"; straight strips or guides used to keep the floating mechanism aligned</note> are placed on the inside, in each of which a channel is carved; and at the top of these, another rule is placed to secure them to the vessel. The two rules with the channels carved into them shall be G.H. and I.K.

the water, its flow through the tube will be constant until all the water in the vessel has exited. But when we desire a faster flow—while still keeping it constant—we shall turn the screw. By pressing down on the water with the basin by means of the frame N.O. L.M., the exit of the water will be made faster than before, and the flow will remain constant. If we want the flow to be even more vigorous, the screw should be turned to lower the cross-member L.M. of the frame, and consequently the basin; if we want it slower, we will raise the basin by turning the screw in the opposite direction. In this way, the flow through the bent tube will be partly constant and partly variable. # ON DRAWING WATER OUT
## Concerning wide tubes. Theorem V.

<image-desc>A woodcut technical illustration showing a hydraulic experiment. On the left is a large, ornate pedestal-mounted vessel (labeled A and B) containing water. A wide, inverted U-shaped siphon pipe extends from inside vessel A/B, arches over the top, and descends into a smaller, similarly ornate vessel (labeled C, D, E, F, G, H) on the right. A horizontal support beam or 'regolo' is shown across the mouth of the larger vessel, through which the siphon tube passes. The smaller vessel on the right is shown with labels indicating its components: the mouth (C), the tube entry (E), and a bottom drainage point (H).</image-desc>

Once the bent tube <term>canna</term> <note>From the Italian for "reed" or "cane," here referring to a siphon pipe.</note> is placed in vessel A.B. with the inner leg submerged in the water and fixed so that it cannot move; by piercing a crossbar <term>regolo</term> <note>A stabilizing bar or rule used to hold the apparatus in place.</note> that traverses the vessel, as a diameter traverses a circle, we shall have another small vessel, not very large, like the one labeled C. D. below. The mouth of this vessel shall be very well sealed with a cover, and in the middle of it, a hole shall be made, and in this, a Pipe E. This pipe must be large enough for the outer leg of the siphon tube to enter it; but inside it, let another Pipe, F. G., be sleeved in leather <note>original: "inuestito di cuoio"</note> and very well tied to E. Let vessel C. D. also be pierced in the bottom at H. Then, fill the vessel with water while plugging the hole H, and let the leather-sleeved Pipe F. G. be fitted onto the outer leg, tying it to the leg very tightly so that no air can enter. And when one wishes to draw the water out of vessel A.B., let the hole H in the bottom of vessel C. D. be opened; as the water exits from it, the air that is in the siphon tube will descend to take its place. This will gradually pull the water from vessel A. B. in such a way that, once vessel C.D. is empty, the air that was in the siphon will have filled that vessel, and the water will have filled the siphon. For the reasons stated above, the siphon will immediately begin its outflow <note>original: "effusione"</note>. Then, having removed vessel C. D., we shall let the siphon flow. For it to operate well, it is necessary that it be upright and very well secured with crossbars, as can be understood from the example described below.

# THAT ONE CAN FILL THE HOLLOW BALL

In the aforementioned manner, the Ball is filled with hot and cold water, or with water and wine, each separated from the other, and one can make now one, now the other come out; or both together at our will in this way. <meta>This page continues the description of Theorem VII from page 16, detailing the operation of a dual-chambered sphere used to dispense two different liquids. It then introduces Theorem VIII, which describes a "Prochita," a vessel used in ancient religious rites to dispense water, wine, or a mixture of both through clever pneumatic control.</meta>

# OF HERO
<note>original header: "DI HERONE"</note>

By plugging one of the holes in the neck and submerging the sphere <note>original: "palla"</note> in the water, the air trapped in that part of the sphere where the tube is closed cannot escape. Therefore, water will only enter the other part, from which the air can escape through the open tube, making room for the water. Once that section is filled with hot water, close its air-hole <term>spiracolo</term> <note>A small vent or opening that allows air to pass, crucial for pneumatic devices.</note> in the neck and remove it from the vessel of hot water. Then, open the other hole and place the sphere in cold water; let it fill in the same manner. Once the second hole is plugged, remove it from the water, and the sphere will be full. 

If you wish to pour out the hot water, open the air-hole or tube of the section where it is contained, and it will flow out through the holes at the bottom of the sphere. When you no longer wish for it to flow, plug that tube again. You may do the same for the part containing the cold water. If you wish to pour both out at once, open both air-holes, and close them when you want the flow to stop. It should be noted that these air-holes can be combined into a single tube divided into two parts; at the top of it, a single hole can be fashioned in such a way that by opening or closing the internal holes as we please, the liquid appears to come from the same single hole in the neck, as the following example demonstrates.

### ON THE VESSEL CALLED PROCHITA, WHICH IN SACRED
#### ministries was used in ancient times. Theorem VIII.
<note>The "Prochita" is a type of ancient Greek vessel, often used for pouring offerings.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut diagram of a specialized vessel called a 'Prochita'. The vessel has a wide body with a decorative handle shaped like a human figure (labeled K) leaning over the top. Vessels are also constructed that, when filled with wine and water, sometimes provide pure water, sometimes pure wine, and sometimes water and wine mixed together. Their construction is done in this way: Let there be a vessel A. B., in the middle of which is placed a diaphragm <term>diafragmama</term> <note>original: "Diafragmama," a partition or membrane.</note>, which is the diameter C. D. Around the body of the vessel, this diameter is pierced with many holes like a sieve <term>cribro</term> or strainer, as we call it. In the middle of this diameter, there is a round hole, through which passes a fitted tube E. G. H., well-attached and sealed at E, with the mouth G a little distance from the bottom of the vessel. The other mouth at H is very well-sealed to the vessel, and a small opening <term>pertugio</term> is made there which enters the mouth of the tube at H upon the "ear" or handle. This handle is made as shown in the figure, being hollow like a pipe, and at its bend at K, another small opening or vent...

# ON THE SPIRITUAL MACHINES
<note>original header: "DEGLI SPIRITALI"</note>

vent <term>spiracolo</term>, which, if plugged with a finger after the vessel is filled with water, will cause the water to remain above the diaphragm <note>original: "Diafragrama"; here referring to a horizontal internal partition or divider.</note> or diameter, unable to descend to the bottom. This is because the air inside the bottom has no other place to escape and yield its space, except through the vents K and H. When these are opened, the water immediately descends through the sieve into the bottom of the vessel. Thus, if we first put wine in the vessel and then close the vent K, and afterwards fill the vessel with water, the water will not mix with the wine. However, by tilting the vessel, only pure water will come out as long as vent K remains closed. Then, by closing the opening H and opening the vent K, only wine will come out through the mouth of the vessel—where the mouth of the internal pipe will reach the level of the vessel's rim. If both are opened, both wine and water will flow out together. It is therefore clear that from this vessel we can pour water and wine, wine alone, or pure water at our will, whenever we wish to play a prank on our friends. The vessel shall be constructed as seen in the figure described above.

# ON THE SPHERE, OR HOLLOW BALL,
->which by itself pushes the water upward. Theorem IX.<-

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut illustration of a pneumatic scientific instrument. It features a large spherical glass or metal vessel (labeled A at the top, B at the bottom) containing water. The sphere sits on an ornate pedestal base (labeled C). A central vertical tube rises from the bottom of the sphere, through the top, and into a shallow basin. Above this basin, the tube branches into a cross-shaped assembly of pipes with various labels (S, L, K, H, F, X, N, M). On the left side of the sphere, a horizontal spout or nozzle with a valve mechanism is attached, labeled T, Y, Q, V. The vessel is partially filled, indicated by horizontal hatching lines representing water.</image-desc>

One may also construct a hollow sphere, or another vessel, from which the water poured inside is discharged and rises by itself with great force until it has all exited, acting against its own nature <note>The "nature" of water, in the Aristotelian sense, is to move downward toward the center of the earth; here, pneumatic pressure overcomes gravity.</note>. It is made in this way: let there be a sphere A.B. of whatever material is most suitable, provided its body is firm and made of such good material that it can resist the great force of the coming compression of the air. The diameter, or width of the body, shall be made according to the will of the maker, whether large, medium, or small. This sphere, placed upon a pedestal <term>hipospario</term> <note>original: "hipospario"; from the Greek *hypospárion*, meaning a base or support stand.</note>—that is, the foot C—shall be perforated at the top. In this hole, a perforated pipe <term>canna</term> is placed, with its interior mouth at a distance from the bottom (directly opposite the hole) that you judge sufficient for the flow of water. You shall raise the pipe somewhat above the ball, soldering it very diligently around the hole so that no air can enter or escape. Then, let this pipe be divided into two tubes D.G. and D.F., into which two other cross-pipes H.K.L.M.N.X. are fitted. These are perforated together with the two tubes D.G. and D.F. Inside the tubes H.K.L.M.N.X., another tube O.P. is inserted, similarly perforated with holes matching those in H.K.L.M.N.X., and this should have the opposite...

# BY HERO

...placed a straight Tube S, similarly perforated with a hole like the others; but let it end in a small mouth at S, as the figure shows. It should be so arranged that when S is taken, the Tube O. P. is turned to close the holes which, when S is turned upwards, correspond to one another so that the water, wishing to exit the vessel, has no way out. After this, let another Tube T. Y. V. be placed into the sphere through some made opening, and let the inner mouth V be plugged; but let it have near the bottom a round hole Q, to which is placed a small valve called by the Latins an *Assarium* <note>original: "Assarium"; a term for a small coin or, in this mechanical context, a flap-valve or check-valve.</note>, which among us is called a *cartella* <note>original: "cartella"; literally "little card," referring to the flap of the valve.</note>, the construction of which I shall explain further below. Then let another Tube Z be made, which enters into the Tube T. Y. V. 

Now, if we pull out Tube Z and pour water into T. Y. V., it will enter the body of the sphere through the opening V, the valve placed on the outside of the tube having opened, while the air yields through the previously mentioned holes of Tube O. P. which are aligned with the holes in tubes H. K. L. M. N. X. When the body of the sphere is half-full of water, you will turn Tube S so that the corresponding holes change position. Then, by moving Tube Z back and forth <note>Describing a pumping action similar to a piston.</note>, you will drive air through it into Tube T. Y. V.; this air will enter the body of the sphere with violence through the valve at hole Q, until it is filled with water and air. Thus, by this violent force, an agitated <term>accumulation of air</term> <note>original: "ammazzamento di aria"; referring to highly compressed air.</note> will be created within. By again pulling out Tube Z so that Tube T. Y. V. fills with air, and then thrusting Tube Z back in and forcing air into the aforementioned ball, and by continuing to do this often, you will come to fill the body of the ball with much air (as if condensed and compressed). This air will not be able to exit, as there is no open vent anywhere, since the valve at hole Q will close by itself. But if you return to lift Tube S so that it stands straight and the holes align, the water will be forced out by the power of the compressed air, which, being altered by its own nature, will push it out with force. If the compressed air is plentiful, it will drive out all the water until the excess air exits along with the water.

## TO PERFORM AN ACTION BY THE FORCE OF A LIGHTED FIRE
### To sacrifice as many animals as we please. Theorem XI.

Animals are made to perform a sacrifice in this way. Let there be a base upon which they can stand, marked A. B. C. D., which is perfectly sealed on all sides. Above this...

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a mechanical altar setup. Two figures stand on a rectangular platform labeled with letters A, B, C, and D. On the left, a woman in flowing robes holds a staff (K) and a circular object. On the right, a bearded man in a long robe and a pointed hat pours liquid from a vessel into a fire burning on a central altar. <note>This experiment is a classic example of Hero's pneumatics. When a fire is lit on the altar, the air inside the pedestal expands. This pressure forces liquid (often wine or water) through hidden pipes, making the statues appear to "pour" a libation or move as if they are performing the ritual themselves.</note>

# BY HERO

...place an altar, similarly sealed on all sides along with the Base, and pierced at G. Through the Base, let as many tubes pass as there are animals; let these be labeled H, I, N, O, positioned a short distance from the bottom as shown at L and N. These tubes should be perforated, as should the arms of the animals, who hold in their hands a vessel or whatever object is to be used for the sacrifice. After this, let water be placed into the Base through a hole, such as at M, which is then immediately plugged. 

Then, let a fire be lit atop the altar E. F. The air enclosed within this altar will be immediately forced by the heat <note>original: "vapor"; in this context, Hero refers to the expansion of air caused by heat, rather than water vapor or steam.</note> to descend into the Base through Tube P and drive out the water. Since the water has no other exit, it will necessarily flow out through the tubes N, O, H, and L, pushed by the force of the heat through the vessels or whatever objects the animals hold in their hands. In this way, the sacrifice is performed. The sacrifice will last as long as the fire remains lit on the altar; when the fire is extinguished, the sacrifice ceases. Thus, it will happen that they will perform the sacrifice as many times as the fire is lit. However, it is necessary that the tube through which the heat must pass be thick in the middle, because a large volume of air is required to have enough force to expel the liquid and allow the mechanism to operate effectively.

### CONCERNING VESSELS WHICH, IF NOT FILLED,
->*do not pour: but when filled, all the liquid within escapes.*<-
### Theorem XII.

Let there be an uncovered vessel A. B. C. D., through the bottom of which is placed a <term>Spiritual Siphon</term> <note>original: "Diabete Spiritale"; a historical term for a siphon mechanism used in pneumatic devices.</note> E. F. G. H., or a bent or curved pipe I. K. L. Then, let the vessel A. B. C. D. be filled with water. For the reasons mentioned above, all the water will flow out until the vessel remains empty, provided that the pipe or "Spiritual Tube" is only as far from the bottom as is necessary for the flow of water.

# CONCERNING THE PNEUMATIC MECHANISMS OF CONCORDANT VESSELS.

### Theorem XIII.

The vessels, which are called concordant <note>original: "vassi concordi"; these are vessels designed to act in unison or "harmony" through interconnected tubes.</note>, are fixed upon a single base. Even if one of them is filled with wine and the other is empty, and although both their channels are open, the wine will not flow out unless the other vessel is filled—let us say, with water. Once the second vessel is filled, both will immediately begin to pour, one water and the other wine; their flow will not cease until they are both completely empty. They are constructed in this manner:

Let the base upon which the vessels are placed be A, B, C, and D, and let the vessels themselves be E and F. Inside each of these, let bent tubes be positioned: in vessel E, place the tube G-H-K, and in vessel F, place the tube L-M-N. Their outlets should lead into curved channels that discharge outside the vessels. The tubes of these vessels are connected by another tube located within the base, labeled O-P-Q-R; the openings O and P should be positioned near the curves of the [siphon] tubes.

<image-desc>A technical diagram illustrating a hydraulic experiment. Two ornate vessels, labeled E and F, sit on a rectangular pedestal base labeled A, B, C, and D. Each vessel contains a liquid and is equipped with a siphon system. Vessel E (left) contains siphon G-H-K; vessel F (right) contains siphon L-M-N. The siphons lead into the base at points O and P and are interconnected through internal piping marked at Q and R. The vessels feature decorative elements including animal-headed spouts or handles.</image-desc>

Next, let one of these vessels be filled with wine—for example, vessel E—but only to a level below the curve of the tube at H. Because the wine does not reach above that curve, it will not flow out, as the tube cannot initiate the flow <note>original: "principio di flusso"; Hero describes the physics of a siphon, which requires the liquid to be high enough to crest the "U" bend before gravity and suction can draw the rest of the liquid out.</note>. However, if we pour enough water into vessel F so that it rises above the curve of the tube M, the water will then begin to flow through the tubes O-P-Q-R into vessel E. This action initiates the flow of the wine; thus, at the same moment, both vessels will pour—one wine and the other water—until all the wine and all the water have exited them.

<meta>catchword: DE (of/concerning)</meta>

<summary>This page describes Theorem XIII, a pneumatic device consisting of two interconnected vessels (concordant vessels) that discharge their contents simultaneously only after the second vessel is filled, using a shared internal tube system to trigger the siphoning process.</summary>
<keywords>Hero of Alexandria, pneumatics, concordant vessels, siphon, hydraulics, wine and water experiment, ancient machinery</keywords>

## CONCERNING VESSELS IN WHICH, BY POURING IN
## Water, a sound or whistle is created. Theorem XIV.

# ON PNEUMATICS

## ON THE DIVERSITY OF SOUNDS
->Concerning various birds. Theorem XV.<-

Although all sounds <note>original: "voci"; literally "voices," referring here to the acoustic output of the machine.</note> are created using pipes, their sounds are rendered differently according to their length, thickness, thinness, and shortness. Furthermore, when parts of them are immersed in water, they produce varied and diverse sounds, mimicking the songs of different birds. These machines are constructed either over fountains, in caves, or wherever is most convenient, provided there is a flow or current of water. One may arrange as many birds as desired; however, for those birds arranged to face a Little Owl <note>original: "Nottola" or "Ciuetta"; names for the *Athene noctua*, a small owl often used in bird-trapping to fascinate smaller birds.</note>—which is designed so that when it turns its face toward the birds, they cease their singing, and when it turns its back, they resume—they are constructed in this manner:

Let a small channel of water be arranged that flows continuously, marked A. Beneath this, place the vessel B. C. D. E., into which is set the pneumatic tube <note>original: "tubo Spiritale"; a pipe designed to move air or "spirit."</note> or the curved pipe F. G. Then, above the large vessel B. C. D. E., place the funnel-vessel H, whose stem should remain as far from the bottom as we deem sufficient for the flow of water. This vessel should have many pipes that pass into the body of the large vessel, which must be very well sealed around the lid,

To the right, a vertical pedestal on a base (MM) supports a mechanical owl (Ciuetta, labeled R S P). A system of pulleys (Q T V), cords, and a small counterweight or bucket (Z) is attached to the pedestal to rotate the owl, which controls the birds' singing through visual cues as described in the text.</image-desc>

as I described in the previous section, and as shown in the example at L. M. While the vessel B. C. D. E. fills with water, the displaced air will exit through the pipes L. M., imitating the song of birds. Each pipe should be fitted into the feet and bodies of the birds in such a way that the sound is sent through their mouths. When the vessel B. C. D. E. becomes full, it will empty itself through the pneumatic tube (the curved pipe), and the birds will cease to sing.

To make the Little Owl turn toward the birds in this instant, as mentioned above: let a rod or straight style be placed, excellently crafted and turned, upon a base MM. This rod should rest on a pivot, marked X, around which is placed the hollow sleeve O. P. (though not entirely pierced through). the aforementioned axle <note>original: "canna"; literally "reed" or "pipe," but here functioning as a mechanical shaft or axle around which a cord is wound.</note> should be wrapped with its ends in opposite directions to one another, and let these be T, Y, V, and Q. But as the vessel B, C, D, E empties into the bucket, and the bucket itself empties through the pneumatic tube that must be placed within it, once the vessel is empty, the weight Z will descend to the bottom. As the axle P, O turns back, the Little Owl will turn away, and at the same time the vessel B, C, D, E will return to being filled with air, and the birds will once again resume their song: until, by emptying, the Little Owl turns once more, and they cease to sing.

# BY THE SAME PRINCIPLE
## *Trumpets are made to sound. Theorem XVI.*

Trumpets are similarly made to sound using the aforementioned principles; for when the funnel <note>original: "infundibulo"</note> is placed into a well-sealed vessel, with its stem placed a short distance from the bottom, and the funnel is extremely carefully sealed with its lid—then, having positioned the mouth of the trumpet (whose reed <note>original: "lingula"; the vibrating tongue of the instrument.</note> and sounding-cup <term>dodoneo<span>the "dodoneum" or sounding chamber of a trumpet, named for the resonant bronze vessels of the oracle at Dodona.</span></term> are joined with the lid of the vessel, which is perforated and well-soldered around it)—it happens that the breath of air cannot exit through any other opening except through the sounding-cup and the reed. Thus, by pouring water into the vessel (which we call the funnel), the air enclosed in the large vessel is forcibly driven out by the water through the reed, forcing the trumpet to sound.

***

# A VESSEL IN WHICH WINE AND WATER ARE POURED,
## *and being kept separate from one another, one can have at*
## *will either pure wine or pure water.*
### Theorem XVIII.

Let there be a vessel A, B, C, in which there are two partitions <note>original: "fondi"; literally 'bottoms,' referring to the horizontal internal dividers.</note> D, H and F, G. In each of these, let there be placed a perforated pipe H, K, which is carefully soldered into each of these partitions. Within this pipe, let a hole L be made slightly above the lower partition F, G, but below the upper partition D, H. Let an air-vent <note>original: "spiracolo"</note> M be made in the body of the vessel. Once everything is thus arranged, and the opening C is plugged, wine is poured into the vessel; this wine will fill the space between the two diaphragms <note>original: "diafragmi"; the internal partitions that divide the vessel into chambers.</note> D, H and F, G by passing through the hole L, because the air previously inside will escape through the vent M. 

When this vent is closed with a finger, the wine that is in the section D, E, F, G will remain there and cannot exit. Then, when water is poured into the upper part of the vessel A, B, D, H while keeping the vent M closed, only pure water will come out. However, if the vent is opened—since there is water in the upper part—when the vessel is tilted, a mixture of wine and water will emerge. Once all the water has been poured out, then the wine will come out pure...

Now, let us also put wine in the base M.N.X.O. and in the cup P.R. so that it is full, and also the base M.N.X.O. is full up to the mouth of the tube G.H. Once this is done, let the mouth E be closed, so that the wine which is in the vessel A.B. will no longer descend into the base through the small tube K.L., as it cannot receive air from anywhere else except through the mouth E, which is already blocked. But when the wine is drawn out

### THAT THE PROPOSED BOWL (ALTHOUGH A
### great quantity of wine or water is drawn from it) shall always remain full. Theorem XX.

Let A.B. be the vessel in which there is sufficient water for future use, and let the channel that comes out of it be C.D. Under this, let another vessel G.H. be placed, and beside the channel, let the rod <note>original: "regolo"; a straight bar or lever used to balance the mechanism.</note> E.F. be placed. From the end E, let the cork <note>original: "souero"; modern Italian 'sughero,' used here as a buoyant float.</note> K be suspended inside the vessel G.H., and from the end F, let a lead weight X be suspended by a cord or small chain. Let it be arranged so that the cork floating in the vessel G.H. closes the mouth of the channel C.D. When drawing the water from G.H., the cork falls with it and opens the mouth of the channel C.D.; and as the vessel G.H. fills up again, the mouth of that channel is blocked once more, whereby the flow of water is stopped. Thus, if the cup is placed in any location whatsoever, the outer rim of which is level with the surface of the water, it will happen that if any—

### A VESSEL IN WHICH, BY DROPPING A FIVE-DRACHMA
### COIN, WATER FLOWS OUT TO SPRINKLE THE ONE WHO INSERTED THE COIN. Theorem XXI.

<image-desc>An illustration of an ornate, lidded urn (labeled A, B, C, D) featuring a cutaway view of its internal mechanical components. At the top is a coin slot (Q).
