Let there be a <term>spondeion<note>original: "spondeo," a sacrificial vessel or urn used for libations.</note></term>—that is, a sacrificial vessel or treasury A. B. C. D.—whose mouth Q. is covered. H. containing water, and within it a <term>pyxis<note>original: "pyxide," a small box or cylindrical container acting here as a valve housing.</note></term> L., from which the channel L. M. exits the vessel. Then, place a straight rod N. X. fixed into the bottom of the vessel; upon this, on a pivot, place another rod O. P. This rod should have a <term>platismatio<note>original: "platišmatio," a Greek term for a flat plate.</note></term> at point O.—or as we say, a wide paddle R.—and it should be parallel to the bottom of the sacrificial vessel. At point P., there shall be a cylinder with a cover, and this cylinder enters the vessel L. so that the water cannot escape through the channel L. M. The cover and the cylinder must be heavier than the plate or paddle (whichever you call it) by exactly the weight of a <term>five-drachma coin<note>original: "moneta di cinque dragme"; the drachma was a silver coin of ancient Greece.</note></term>—

# HOW TO PLACE VARIOUS SORTS OF WINE 
## white, red, of many flavors, and water, and make them at our will exit through a single
### channel. Theorem XXII.

Let there be a sealed vessel A.B., closed at the neck by a <term>diaphragm<note>A horizontal dividing plate used to control the flow of air or liquid.</note></term> C.D. Throughout the height of the vessel, there should be as many diaphragms or partitions as there are <term>liquids<note>original: "humori" (humors), a common Renaissance term for any fluid.</note></term> you wish to put into said vessel. These must be very well soldered to the body of the vessel and to the diaphragm C.D. For easier understanding, we will say there are two, namely:

<image-desc>A detailed technical engraving of a pneumatic vessel or ewer. The vessel is ornate, featuring a pedestal base decorated with a human face and side ornaments resembling lion heads. It has an intricate handle. Letters are used to label specific components for a scientific demonstration: 'A' and 'B' at the top opening; 'C' and 'D' identifying a perforated plate or diaphragm in the neck; 'E' and 'F' on the main body of the vessel; 'G', 'H', and 'K' labeling small ventilation tubes (spiracoli) around the shoulder; and 'R' at the bottom discharge spout.</image-desc>

E. and F. Let it also be arranged so that the diaphragm C.D. has as many holes as it can contain, like a <term>sieve<note>original: "criuello" or "cribro." A perforated plate that allows liquid to pass through while managing air flow.</note></term>, dense and small, which lead to all the spaces between the partitions. Under the diaphragm are the <term>vent-holes<note>original: "spiracoli," literally "little breathers." These are small air vents that the user covers with their fingers to control the flow.</note></term> G.H.K., which pass into the sections where the liquids are to be poured. From these sections, perforated pipes emerge, soldered to said partitions, so that they all enter into one common channel R; however, they are soldered to the partitions in such a way that the liquids do not mix. 

If you close the vent-holes G.H.K. <note>The OCR reads "B" here, but context suggests "K" as per the diagram.</note> and the channel R, and place either water, wine, or whatever sort of liquid you please into the mouth of the vessel, it will not descend into any section, because the air enclosed within them has no exit. But, if you open one of the said vent-holes, immediately the water or wine you placed in the mouth above will enter the space where the breath of air is permitted. Then, if that vent is closed and another vent-hole opened, 

and another sort of liquid is placed therein, it will similarly descend into the part where the vent is open. Therefore, once all the vent-holes and the holes of the sieve are closed, even if you open the mouth of the channel R, nothing will come out unless you unblock a vent-hole. When air enters through it, the liquid contained in that specific section will flow out. When this is closed and another similar one is opened, the same will happen, and so on for all the others.

## IF THERE BE WATER IN A VESSEL, AND IN IT
### *the channel in which there is a tap, and in said water an animal floats; to make it so that as much water as is drawn from the vessel, an equal amount of wine sprays from the mouth of the animal. Theorem XXV.*

Let A. B. be the water vessel, at the bottom of which is the metal-lined <term>channel<note>original: "ferrato canale"; likely a reinforced pipe or a tap housing.</note></term> C. And in this water let the bowl D. float, in which there is the tube E. F. shaped into an animal. Then, beside the said vessel, let the vessel G. H. be placed, filled with wine, in which there is the bent <term>pipe<note>original: "canna"; used throughout for tubes, pipes, or reeds.</note></term> K. L. M.; one leg of which is in vessel G. H., and the other enters the tube E. F. If we draw the wine through the mouth M., it will flow into the tube E. F., and it will not stop until the surface of the wine in vessel G. H. is leveled in the same line as that of the wine in the tube E. F. Let these, then, be in a straight line N. X. P.

And in the tube let there be the small open channel R.; until this point, the wine will not flow out of it. X. will become lower than the surface of the wine; wherefore, as the leg of the bent pipe that is in the tube E. F. becomes lower <note>As the float drops, the siphon's discharge point moves below the wine level in the reservoir, triggering the flow.</note>, the wine will exit through the channel R. # OF PNEUMATICS

## BUT IF WE SHOULD LIKE TO SEE AS MUCH
### wine come out as there is water in a vessel, it shall be arranged thus. Theor. XXVI.

Once again, let vessel A. B. be full of water, and vessel G. H. be full of wine. But let the tube E. E. be outside the vessel A. B., and in said vessel A. B. let the <term>sphere<note>original: "ſphera"; a floating ball used as a sensor for the water level.</note></term> D. float, from which a rope originates that passes through the two <term>pulleys<note>original: "girelle"; small wheels used to redirect the force of the rope.</note></term> S. T. and is attached to the tube E. E. so that it remains suspended. For the rest, let everything remain according to the reasons stated above; thus, if we pour water into vessel A. B., the sphere or ball will rise, and consequently lower the tube E. E. As it lowers, the wine will flow out through it.

<image-desc>A woodcut technical illustration showing a hydraulic system mounted on a wooden frame. On the left, a cylindrical vessel (marked B) contains water and a floating sphere. A rope attached to the sphere goes up to a pulley (T) on the crossbeam, travels horizontally to another pulley (S), and then drops down to support a movable tube assembly (labeled L, E, O, X, P). This assembly is positioned over a taller central vessel (marked G and H) containing wine. Liquid is shown flowing from a spout (R) into a small cup, demonstrating how the rising water level in the first vessel causes the discharge of wine from the second.</image-desc>

In this other way, the same thing can also be done: let the rope from which the sphere D. is suspended pass through the <term>pulley<note>original: "troclea"; a classical term for a pulley or block-and-tackle.</note></term> S. and be directed to the other pulley T. Passing through this, let it be tied to the bent pipe. It will then happen that as the sphere D. rises, the bent pipe suspended by the rope will be lowered, and by lowering, it will consequently pour out as much wine as the amount of water placed in the vessel where the ball floats on the surface.

# IN PLACES WHERE RUNNING WATER IS FOUND
*To construct an animal, made of copper or whatever other*
*material desired, which cries out continuously; however, if a basin*
*of water is brought to it, it drinks without noise, and having drunk, it returns*
*to its crying once more. Theorem XXVIII.*

Let the vessel be A.B., into which the flow of water falls through the small channel C. Inside it, let there be the bent tube D.E.F., or a <term>spiral siphon<note>original: "diabete spirale"; a term derived from the Greek word for a siphon or a drafting tool, used here to describe a curved pipe that moves liquid by atmospheric pressure.</note></term>, of which the long leg extends below the bottom of the vessel. Beneath this shall be placed the base G.H., sealed excellently all around, which also has within its body a spiral <term>siphon<note>original: "diabete spirale, ò inflessa siffone"; Hero's machines often use siphons to create intermittent actions or cycles.</note></term>

M. N. X. And under the tube D.E.F., let the <term>funnel<note>original: "infundibulo"; from the Latin 'infundibulum,' a vessel for pouring.</note></term> O.P. be placed, of which the bottom, being narrowed into a point, enters into the base G.H. However, let the point of it be as distant from the bottom as the flow of water seems to require. Upon the base, let there be the animal R. Inside its body, let a tube pass either through a foot or some other part, hidden in such a way that no one notices it, and let it pass secretly into the base; let this [tube] be R.T. When the vessel A.B. is full of water, this will fall through the bent tube D.E.F. into the funnel O.P., and the base G.H. will fill, and the vessel A.B. will empty. While the water falling from the funnel O.P. fills the base G.H., the air that is within it will exit through the mouth R <note>Generating a whistling or "crying" sound as the air is forced out by the rising water.</note>. But once the base is filled by the overflowing liquid, it will empty itself through the bent tube M.N.X.

and while it empties, the air will again enter through the mouth **R.**, filling that space which the water goes on yielding to it; whence it will happen that if we offer a cup full of water to the mouth of the animal **R.**, it will suck it up; because, as was said above, no vacuum is allowed in the things of nature <note>This refers to the Aristotelian concept of *horror vacui*, the belief that nature will immediately fill any empty space with matter, which Hero uses to explain the suction created by air displacement.</note>, so that the water will be drawn by the violence of the air into the base through the mouth **R.** until the base is entirely exhausted. ## HOW WITHOUT THE FLOW OF WATER, OR TURNING A KEY
### *the aforementioned Animal may be made to drink. Theorem XXX.*

Suppose we have a base **A. B. C. D.**, and the mouth of the animal is at **E.**, through the breast of which, and through one of the feet (or through the tail), is placed the perforated pipe **E. H. G.** with one end fixed in the interior part of the base. This pipe remains fixed and immobile in the base; and the tube or pipe **E. H. G.** which, as I have said, will pass

<image-desc>A woodcut diagram showing a bird standing on a rectangular box labeled with letters A, B, C, D. A dotted line traces an internal pipe (E.H.G.) that runs from the bird's beak (E), through its head (H), and down into the box (G).</image-desc>

through the animal with a small, and barely visible hole, shall be pierced at **H.** What will happen is that if someone, by means of some tube above, the end of which is in the hole where **H.** is, fills this tube **E. H. G.**, it will remain full; because the mouths of it at **E.** and **G.** are on a perfect plane <note>Meaning the two ends of the pipe are at the same horizontal level.</note>, and **H.** is most precisely pierced in the middle. Thus, if the cup is removed from **H.** and we incline one end of the bent pipe more than the other—let us say **G.**—it will follow that as the part of the pipe at **G.** becomes greater, it will have the power to draw the water, which will be carried into the base **A. B. C. D.** And for this reason, it is not necessary for the base to be closed on all sides.

### *THROUGH THE MOUTH OF A VESSEL, MANY TYPES OF WINE CAN BE PLACED WITHIN, AND THROUGH THAT SAME CHANNEL, EACH CAN BE DRAWN OUT AT THE PLEASURE OF THE ONE WHO CHOOSES WHICH THEY WANT; INDEED, EVEN IF MANY PEOPLE PUT MANY SORTS OF WINE INSIDE, EACH PERSON CAN HAVE THEIR OWN, AND SPECIFICALLY AS MUCH OF EACH AS WAS PUT THEREIN.*

->Theorem XXXII.<-

Let there be a sealed vessel **A. B. C. D.**, which has a neck partitioned by a diaphragm <term>diaphragm [diafragrama]: a horizontal partition or plate used to divide chambers</term> **E. F.** Let the vessel also be divided by partitions into as many parts as there are types of wine necessary to put inside; for example, let the diaphragms or partitions be **C. D.** and **G. H.**, so that three spaces are separated from one another. In these, the wine can be placed. However, the diaphragm **E. F.** must be pierced in alignment with each part of the vessels or spaces distinguished by the partitions **C. D.** and **G. H.** with thick and tiny holes <note>These act like a sieve to allow liquid to pass while managing air flow.</note>. Furthermore, let three holes **O. P. R.** be made, from which rise the tubes **P. S.**, **Q. T.**, and **R. V.** into the neck, which is perforated by them. Around each tube in the diaphragm **E. F.**, let there be tiny holes in the manner of a sieve or strainer <note>original: "cribro, ò criuello"</note> through which the water or wine enters into its own designated place. When we wish to fill them with any wine, we shall close the air-vents <term>air-vents [ſpiracoli]: small tubes that allow air to escape so liquid can enter</term> **S. T. V.** with our fingers; then, once the wine is placed in the neck of the vessel, because the air contained in the said spaces will have no exit, the wine will not descend into any space until we unblock the aforementioned air-vents

### TO CONSTRUCT A LAMP
->Which advances itself as it is consumed. Theorem XXXIII.<-

<image-desc>A technical illustration of a mechanical oil lamp. The lamp body (A, B, C) contains a floating basin (G) attached to a vertical toothed rack (H). This rack engages with a gear (F), which is connected to a toothed lever (D, E) that holds the wick. As the oil level drops, the basin G sinks, causing the rack to turn the gear and advance the wick (stoppino) automatically. The wick is shown lit with a flame on the left side.</image-desc>

Let there be a Lamp **A. B. C.** In its mouth, let there be an iron link <note>original: "fibbia"; here referring to a toothed rack or mechanical linkage</note> **D. E.** which moves freely at point **E**, and upon or around this link let the wick <term>stoppino|The cord of fiber that draws oil to the flame</term> be wound, but in such a way that it can easily slide. Then, let a small toothed wheel **F** <note>original: "ruletto dentato"</note> move readily around its axle, and let its teeth engage the teeth of the link; but in such a way that as it turns, the wick is pushed forward by the teeth of the link. The Lamp must have a body of a conveniently large size. When oil is poured into it, a basin **G** shall float therein, to which another [toothed rack] is also fixed, but in such a way that its teeth are engaged with those of the small wheel. As the oil is consumed, the basin will sink, and as it sinks, its teeth will turn the small wheel **F**, and in this manner the wick will advance by itself.

If the air hole is not closed, the water will simply descend into the body of the vessel and will not flow through the channel until the air hole is closed again. Once the vessel is full and the air hole is reset, for the reasons cited elsewhere, all the liquid will exit.

# TO CONSTRUCT A VESSEL IN WHICH
->by pouring liquid in, it will be received; but if one stops pouring,<-
->it will receive no more water. Theorem XXXV.<-

Let the neck of the vessel be **A.B.**, closed with a diaphragm <term>diafragramma|A horizontal partition or divider inside the vessel</term> **C.D.** through which passes the tube **E.F.**. One end of this tube should be a short distance from the bottom of the vessel, and the other end should be above the partition, or diaphragm, almost level with the lip of the vessel. Around this, let there be another tube **G.H.**, placed at such a distance from the first tube and the diaphragm as is sufficient for the flow of water, as was said in the second [theorem] of this book. Let the part of this tube **G.H.** be formed with a square fitting <unclear>to</unclear>, and let the vessel have an air hole **K** which opens into its body. When we pour water into the neck, it will happen that the water descends into the body of the vessel through tube **G.H.** and out through **E.F.**, while the air that was inside exits through the air hole **K**. If we then stop pouring water and the neck of the vessel becomes empty, and if the air hole is closed, the air will break its continuity to return to its natural thinness: whereby

### UPON A BASE A SATYR MAY BE PLACED,
> *Who holds a <term>wineskin|vtre; an animal-skin bag used for liquids</term> in his hands, beneath which there is a <term>basin|auello; a small trough or reservoir</term> filled with water. ->*Theorem XXXVI.*<-

Afterward, place another tube **K.L.**, which similarly passes through the cover of the vessel and stands just above the partition, a short distance from it; but let it be excellently soldered to the said cover, above which it rises as can be seen. The outflow of water shall fall into the basin, which from it

A funnel and several internal tubes (labeled F, K, L, M, H) facilitate the movement of air and water between the compartments. Labels N and M mark specific points of entry and exit.</image-desc>

and entering the vessel **A. D.**, it will force the water contained therein to enter the tube **K. L.** and fall into the basin through the tube. From there, it is carried again into the vessel **B. C.**, which similarly forces out the air contained within it. This air again compels the water in vessel **A. D.** to fall by force into the basin. This motion will continue until all the water in vessel **A. D.** has been emptied. It will therefore be necessary to arrange the tube **K. L. M.** so that it passes through the mouth of the <term>wineskin|vtre; an animal-skin bag used for liquids</term>, and that the mouth **M.** is small enough that this motion lasts for a significant time.

## HOW TO CONSTRUCT AN ALTAR UPON WHICH,
## once a fire is lit, the doors of a Temple suddenly open, and when the fire is
## extinguished, they immediately close again. Theorem XXXVII.

Upon a base **A. B. C. D.**, let the altar **E. O.** be constructed, through which the tube **E. G.** passes. The mouth **E.** shall be within the body of the altar <note>The fire on top of the altar heats the air inside this tube</note>, and the mouth **G.** shall lead into a concave or hollow sphere—as we might call it—labeled **H.** This sphere should be soldered not on its perpendicular diameter, but somewhat distant from it. Then, place the bent <term>siphon|siffone; a curved tube used to move liquid from a higher container to a lower one using air pressure</term> **K. L. M.** into said sphere. Let the <term>hinges|cardini; the vertical pivots or pins on which the doors turn</term> of the doors be extended into the lower part of the base; these must turn very easily upon their centers, which are at the bottom of the base **A. B. C. D.** Around these hinges, some ropes or small chains should be wound or wrapped, passing through the <term>pulley|troclea; a wheel used to change the direction of a pulling force</term> **P.**, and they shall support the hollow vessel **N. X.** Also, other chains should be wound around the same hinges in the opposite direction to those mentioned above; one end of these should pass through the pulley and support the weight **R.**, which, in descending, closes the doors. Make it so that the bent siphon has its outer leg inside the suspended vessel **X. N.**, and in the sphere there shall be a hole **Z.** through which it is filled halfway with water; let this hole be plugged immediately:

<summary>This page concludes the description of a self-sustaining fountain driven by air displacement and introduces a famous mechanism where the heat from an altar fire expands air to drive water into a bucket, pulling the temple doors open via a system of pulleys and weights.</summary>

<keywords>Hero of Alexandria, hydraulics, pneumatic machines, temple doors, automated mechanisms, Renaissance engineering, siphon, air pressure, thermal expansion</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Theorem XXXVII, regarding the automated temple doors, following the heading provided at the end of the previous page.</meta>

->### OF HERON<-

hole, <note>This likely refers to a small aperture in the altar base mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> so that while the fire lit upon the altar burns, it will force the air—which is within the body of the altar—to enter the sphere through the tube **F.G.** This air, entering the sphere, will force the water to exit through the <term>siphon|siffone; a bent tube used to move liquid from one container to another</term> **K. L. M.** and fall into the empty vessel suspended by the rope (or small chain) that passes over the <term>pulley|troclea; a mechanical wheel used to change the direction of a pulling force</term> **P.** Once this vessel is filled with water—because every heavy thing tends toward the bottom—it will move downward, pulling the rope. By the force of this rope, the hinges will be turned and the doors will open. But when the fire is extinguished once more, the air—having been thinned—will exit through the <term>porosity|rarità; refers here to the cooling air contracting or escaping</term> of the sphere's body, and the curved siphon **K.L.M.** will draw the water back out of the suspended vessel, and it will return again into the ball (or hollow sphere). This occurs because the end of the outer leg **M.** will be immersed in the water contained in the suspended vessel. It will then happen that as the vessel empties, and is thereby made lighter, the weight **R.** will descend to the bottom and close the doors, which is the result that was proposed.

There are some who use <term>mercury|hidargiro; from the Greek 'hydrargyros' or 'liquid silver'</term> instead of water, because it is heavier than water and is easily affected by heat.

### YET ANOTHER WAY IN WHICH, ONCE A FIRE IS LIT
### upon an Altar, the proposed doors are made to open.

<margin>Theorem 38.</margin>
Let the door stand above the base **A.B.C.D.**, upon which let there be the altar **E.**; and through the altar let the tube **F.G.H.** pass and terminate in the <term>vessel|vtre; literally a leather skin or bladder, used here as an airtight expandable bag</term> **K.**, which must be very well sealed on all sides. Beneath this, let the weight **L.** be placed, which is suspended by a rope or chain by means of a <term>pulley|girella; a small rotating wheel</term>, hanging from the ropes or chains wound around the <term>hinges|cardini; the vertical pivots on which the doors turn</term>, as seen in the figure. Thus, as the vessel descends, the weight **L.** drops; in dropping down, it will pull the ropes or chains which, by winding around the hinges, close the doors. But when the fire is lit upon the altar, they will open; because the air within the body of the altar, driven out by the heat of the fire, will descend into the vessel through the tube **F.G.H.** and will draw <note>The text implies the expanding air inflates or moves the vessel in a way that counteracts the closing weight.</note> it to itself, and with it the weight **L.**, whereby the doors will open. Alternatively, one may make them as the doors of the <term>Baths|Bagni; referring to Roman-style public baths which often featured automated or self-closing doors</term> are usually made, so that they close by themselves, or have a counter-weight that opens them; because when the fire is extinguished, the air that entered the vessel will return to its place: whereupon as the vessel descends, and with it the weight, the said doors will be closed.

## A VESSEL FILLED WITH WINE, WHICH HAS
-> three channels, arranged so that from the middle one wine flows, and when water <-
-> is added to the vessel, the flow of wine stops; but the water <-
-> flows out through the other two channels, and once the water stops, <-
-> the wine returns to flow out, and this may happen as <-
-> many times as we please. <-

### *Theorem* XXXIX.

**L**et the Vessel be **A. B.** which has its neck crossed by a <term>diaphragm|diafragramma; an airtight internal partition or plate</term> **C. D.**, and in the bottom of said vessel let there be a small outlet **E.** Then, let it be arranged so that through the diaphragm pass two tubes **F. M.** and **K. O.** be placed, covered with a <term>cap|squama; literally a "scale," here referring to a small lid or plate</term> at the top. From the surface of the diaphragm, let these tubes—

—stand as far apart as seems sufficient for the flow of water (a <term>bent siphon|inflessa sissone</term> will also produce this effect). —is filled by us with water, the air can no longer enter; thus the wine can no longer flow out (for the reasons stated elsewhere <note>Hero refers to the principle that liquid cannot leave a sealed container unless air enters to take its place</note>). G.** begins and goes to join at **G.**, which is the "ear" <note>The handle of the vessel.</note> of said vessel; this handle will be hollow like the tube. E.**, provided there is a hole in the vessel for the air to escape. Likewise, the sphere will fill with liquid. If, therefore (which is our purpose), we pour from the vessel while opening the vent **H.**, the liquid contained in the sphere will come out through the tube **D. E.**, and if again...

## IN YET ANOTHER WAY, AT
### *various intervals, different songs of various birds are produced*
### *in this manner. Theorem 44.*

<image-desc>A detailed technical diagram showing a rectangular hydraulic apparatus designed to simulate bird songs. The vessel is divided into four horizontal compartments by diaphragms. Inverted U-shaped siphons (labeled 'diabeti' or 'siffone') connect each level to the one below. At the top, a funnel (infõdibulo) labeled 'A' is placed beneath a decorative face that appears to blow water or air into it. Several pipes (canne) with leafy tops emerge from the upper levels. The system uses falling water to displace air and produce sounds. The final outlet siphon is labeled 'H' at the bottom.</image-desc>

Let a vessel be made, closed on all sides and partitioned with various diaphragms <term>diaphragms|Horizontal plates or dividers that split the internal space of the vessel into multiple airtight chambers.</term>; and in each part let there be placed either curved siphons or pneumatic siphons <note>original: "diabeti spiritali." In ancient mechanics, a 'diabetes' referred to a specific type of siphon used to regulate fluid levels.</note> that carry the water from one place to another as has been described elsewhere. In each diaphragm, let there pass one or more hollow pipes, soldered to them and so adapted that with the breath <note>original: "fiato." In this context, it refers to the air being displaced and forced through the pipes by the rising water.</note> of air they produce a whistling sound. This sound will vary if the pipes are of different thicknesses and lengths.

Then, having placed the funnel atop the vessel, let its stem be as far from the first diaphragm as will suffice for the flow of the water; so that as the water falls into the funnel through channel **A**, it will enter the first vessel above the first diaphragm, driving out the air through the first set of pipes, which will produce various birdsongs. Once this chamber is full, the vessel will empty itself into the second through the curved siphon, performing the same action there, and likewise in the third and the others, until in the final part the siphon or curved siphon sends the water out. Each pipe placed in any part of the vessel will thus yield its designated sound.

As each chamber fills, it forces air through whistles of varying sizes before automatically draining into the next level to trigger a new sound.</summary>

# TO MAKE LIGHT, HOLLOW BALLS
->*dance in this manner.*<-
### Theorem XLV.

<image-desc>An ornate, decorative urn or vessel with a face on its front and scroll-like handles. A vertical tube extends from the top of the vessel, and a small ball is shown balanced or bouncing at the top of the pipe's opening.</image-desc>

When a basin full of water is heated, and its mouth is covered, a tube or pipe should extend above the cover. At the opening of this pipe, another smaller basin is placed, shaped like a hemisphere <note>original: "mezza sphera."</note>. This pipe, along with the cover and the hemisphere, must be perforated. If we place a light or hollow ball in this basin at the top of the pipe, it will happen that the steam <note>original: "vapore." In Hero’s mechanics, this refers to the heated air and water vapor expanding.</note> which must rise through the tube due to the heat below will lift the ball, so that it will appear to dance to anyone who pays attention to it.

# AND TRANSPARENT SPHERES, WHICH
->*contain within themselves both air and water, and in the center a ball, like*<-
->*the Earth in the center of the Universe; they are made in this way.*<-
### Theorem XLVI.

<image-desc>A circular diagram depicting a transparent sphere. The bottom half of the circle is filled with wavy lines representing water, while the top half represents air. A small circle (a ball or "sferula") is positioned in the center, between the air and water sections.</image-desc>

Let two glass hemispheres <term>hemispheres|Half-spheres or bowl-shaped glass pieces.</term> be manufactured, one of which is covered with a very thin sheet of metal; in the center of this sheet, there should be a round hole. Then, let a smaller but light sphere be made. After putting water into the other hemisphere and placing the small sphere <note>original: "sferula."</note> inside it, the two glass hemispheres should be joined together. The liquid <note>original: "l'humido."</note> that the small sphere receives will hold it in the empty space. By joining these two hemispheres together, the proposed effect will be achieved.

## HOW MOISTURE MAY DRIP DROP BY DROP, DRIVEN 
*by the penetrating rays of the Sun. Theorem XLVII.*

Let there be a base, closed all around, marked A, B, C, D, into which the funnel <note>original: "infondibulo"</note> H is placed by its stem; but let the end of this stem be some distance from the bottom. Then, let a sphere or vessel E. F. be made, through which a tube passes from the bottom of the base,

<image-desc>A technical woodcut diagram of a hydraulic scientific apparatus. A rectangular basin base (labeled A, B, C, D) contains a central vertical tube (H). On top of the base is a glass sphere (E. F.) partially filled with liquid. A curved tube (G) connects the sphere's top to a separate bowl-like vessel (II) sitting on the same base. The illustration demonstrates thermal expansion or condensation caused by solar heat.</image-desc>

and its ends should be at some distance from the top of the sphere. Afterwards, let a siphon <note>original: "siffone"</note> be placed in the sphere and soldered very well to it with one leg, while the other leg falls into the funnel. Then let water be put into the sphere; when the heat of the Sun enters the said sphere, the air heated within will drive out the moisture, which will be carried through the bent pipe G and through the funnel H into the base A. B. C. D. But when the base is covered by shade (as the air contracts) <note>The text says "partendo l'aria," literally "the air leaving," referring to the cooling air losing volume and creating a vacuum.</note>, the tube in the sphere will take up the moisture and fill the empty space, and this will happen as many times as the Sun enters it.

## BY SUBMERGING IN WATER THE FOOTLESS VESSEL
*called a Thyrsus, to make a sound come forth, either of a pipe or of some bird. Theorem XLVIII.*

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a tall, ornate ceramic vessel called a 'Thirso'. It features a decorative hammer-like handle on the left and various labels (A, B, C, D, E, F) marking internal and external points. A narrow tube (F) is located near the mouth. The diagram shows how air pressure creates sound as the vessel is submerged.</image-desc>

Let the proposed Thyrsus <term>Thyrsus|A name for a specific type of tall, staff-like vessel, named after the ritual wand of Bacchus.</term> be A. B. C. D., which has a hole at the tip of the bottom; but let this tip be somewhat concave like a Pinecone, and let its neck, somewhat below the mouth, be partitioned with a diaphragm <note>original: "diafragrama"</note> A. E., in which the small pipe F is placed, located below the mouth of the tube, and perforated along with the diaphragm itself. So, when we submerge this Thyrsus in water by pushing it down, the air that is within it (being driven out) will create the proposed sound as it exits through the pipe—if the pipe is alone. But if there is a quantity of water above the diaphragm A. E., it will make a gurgling <note>original: "strepitoso," meaning noisy or clamorous, here describing the sound of air through water.</note> sound, which is the proposed method.

# TO MAKE A STATUE, WHICH IS PLACED
### *upon a base, and which has a Trumpet at its mouth, to sound,*
### *by us providing breath in any of the aforementioned ways.*

->Theorem XLIX.<-

Let the base be closed on all sides, marked A.B.C.D., upon which the Statue is placed, or any other animal according to our whim. And within the base let there be a hollow and sealed hemisphere <note>original: "emisperio"</note> E.F.G., which has several small holes at the bottom: from this, let the tube H.F. pass into the Statue, leading into the mouth of the Trumpet: which

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of a mechanical device. A statue of a male figure wearing a tunic, cloak, and feathered cap stands upon a circular platform (F), which is mounted on a larger rectangular pedestal marked at its corners with the letters A, B, C, and D. however, should be fitted with its reed <note>original: "lingula," the vibrating tongue that produces the sound</note> and with its sound-chamber <note>original: "dodoneo," likely referring to a resonator or mouthpiece assembly named after the resonant bronze vessels of Dodona</note>. In the base, let water be poured through a hole E, which after the filling is to be sealed with every care with a small plate <note>original: "assario"</note> or plug <note>original: "cartella"</note> as was said above. Then, by forcing air into the base, it will happen that the water, rising into the hemisphere through the holes made, will drive out the air through the pipe F.H., which will without fail provide breath to the Trumpet. And when one stops forcing air into the base, the water that rose into the hemisphere will fall back into the base through the same holes, and the air that escaped through the mouth of the same Trumpet will return into it.

### A VESSEL FILLED WITH WATER BEING HEATED
### *to make a hollow sphere rotate upon two poles. Theorem L.*

<image-desc>A woodcut diagram of a steam-powered apparatus. A large cylindrical vessel (A, B) with ornate handles sits on a decorative base. A lid (C, D) covers the vessel. A tube (E, F, G) rises from the lid and enters a hollow sphere (H, K) from one side. On the opposite side, the sphere is supported by a rod (L, M) attached to the lid. Let the vessel filled with water and heated be A. B. Its mouth is to be carefully sealed with a lid C. D. Then, let it be pierced by the bent tube E. F. G., of which the extremity G. is to be carefully inserted into the hollow sphere <term>H. K.</term> <note>This is the famous "Aeolipile," considered the first recorded steam engine.</note> And at the opposite point of this diameter of the sphere, let a pole L. M. be placed, also bent like the tube E. F. G. and fixed into the lid of the vessel C. D. The sphere should have two bent tubes, one diametrically opposite the other, and pierced through so that their holes correspond, and their bends should be at right angles. It will then happen that once the vessel is heated, the steam <note>original: "vapore"</note> will rise into the sphere through the tube E. F. G. and will exit through the bent tubes. This will cause the sphere to whirl around in the same manner as those artificial dances of animals <note>original: "balli di animali," likely referring to mechanical automata or carousel-like toys popular in the period</note> are sometimes seen to revolve.

# TO CONSTRUCT A SMALL GOURD OR CUPPING-GLASS,
## that draws without fire. Theorem LVI.

Let the <term>cupping-glass</term> <note>original: "cucurbitula, ò ventosa"; a bell-shaped vessel used in ancient medicine to create a vacuum on the skin to draw out fluids or "humors." Traditionally, this vacuum was created by heating the air inside with a flame.</note> **A.B.C.** be made in the usual manner, which shall have a **diaphragm** <note>original: "diafragrama"; a partition or dividing wall.</note> **D.E.** in the middle, and a <term>synerisma</term> <note>original: "simerisma" or "schizzo"; here referring to a specialized rotating valve or syringe-like mechanism used to control the flow of air or liquid.</note> (or syringe, as we call it) at the bottom. The outer tube of this shall be **F.G.** and the inner one **H.K.**, with holes **L.M.** that align exactly with one another. Let these parts of the syringe be in that section that protrudes outside of the cupping-glass. The internal holes of these tubes should be open, but the external holes of tube **H.K.** should be closed, and this tube should have a handle.

In addition to this, another <term>synerisma</term> or syringe, similar to the one described above, should be made beneath the diaphragm. This one should also have holes near the bottom which, as in the other, align within the cupping-glass, and let them be bored through together with the diaphragm **D.E.** Once these are fitted, the inner tubes are turned by their handles so that the apertures align; however, those located beneath the diaphragm **D.E.** remain closed when turned, so that when the vessel **C.D.** is filled with air

...the entrance having been closed, which through the pipe F.G. allowed air into the vessel: wherefore the wine through C will necessarily stop; then, having poured into this vessel another measure of water, which rises above the tube H, it will necessarily exit through the two channels M and N. ### THAT A VESSEL FULL OF WINE, HAVING A CHANNEL THROUGH IT, WILL SOMETIMES POUR WINE; AND BY POURING WATER INTO IT, IT WILL POUR PURE WATER; THEN AGAIN IT WILL POUR WINE, AND IF ONE PREFERS, IT WILL POUR WATER AND WINE MIXED TOGETHER. Theorem LIX.

<image-desc>A schematic woodcut illustration of a classical ornate vessel (amphora) with two handles and a decorative base. A cutaway view reveals internal components labeled with letters: a horizontal diaphragm (C-D) separates the neck from the body; a central vertical tube (E-F) runs through it to an external spout at the base; a small aperture (G) is located near the base of the internal tube; and an air vent (H) is positioned on the vessel's shoulder. The body is labeled A.</image-desc>

If, for example, there is a vessel A.B. whose neck is divided by a <term>diaphragm|A horizontal partition or internal wall used to separate chambers</term> C.D., through which passes the tube E.F. that has its outlet at the bottom. In G, there is a small hole inside the body of the vessel, a short distance from the bottom. Below the neck, there is an <term>air vent|A small hole or "spiracolo" used to regulate internal air pressure</term> H. If we close the channel F and pour wine into the vessel, it will enter the belly of the vessel as the air makes room by escaping through the vent H. If the vent is closed, the wine will not come out, except for what remains in the tube E.F. Thus, if we place pure water in the neck of the vessel, it will flow out; but by opening the vent <note>The source text says "N," but context suggests it refers to the vent "H" or a related control point</note>, the water will come out mixed with the wine. Once the water is finished, only pure wine will flow.

## **HOW TO MAKE A VESSEL, FULL OF WINE, STANDING UPON** *a base, with an open channel in the bottom, such that by lowering a weight the channel pours the wine by measure: that is, at our pleasure, a pitcher at times, and at other times half a pitcher, and finally as much as you like.*

Once this is done, place at the end of this lever at P the vessel Z, suspended with a rope or chain, in the bottom of which is the hole T. But before the water is placed in the base, let the vessel be filled through tube G.H., which can be done [by]

...as the air escapes through tube O. X.; as long as the mouth O. of the tube O. X. is closed and the channel D. is opened, there is no doubt that the wine will not flow out, for the reasons mentioned elsewhere. However, if we lower the end of the <term>lever</term> <note>Original: "regola." A straight-edged bar or rod used here as a balance beam or ruler.</note> at R., the part of the vessel hanging from the other end of the lever at P. will be raised. And because water has entered the vessel through the hole T., as the vessel is lifted, the water is removed from the base, and thus a little "respiration" <note>In pneumatic engineering, "respiration" refers to the intake of air to replace displaced liquid, thereby breaking a vacuum and allowing flow.</note> is given to the mouth O., so that the wine will exit from the channel. As long as the water leaves the vessel through the hole T., the mouth of the tube O. will once again be blocked. Likewise, if we return to lowering the lever R. further than we did before, a greater quantity of wine will flow through channel D. But if we raise the entire vessel, the mouth D. will pour out a much greater quantity of wine. To avoid the effort of depressing the lever R. by hand, let the weight Q. be attached to the outer part of the lever R. When this weight is at R., it will lift the entire vessel out of the water; the closer it is moved toward the base, the smaller the quantity of wine will exit through channel D. Thus, having discovered through experience the amounts we wish to depress the lever R. to obtain various quantities of wine, we shall mark them upon the lever. Then, by moving the weight to whichever mark we please, we will have the desired quantity of wine at our will, always opening and closing channel D.

## ALEOTTI'S NOTE.

One can also do this without using water, if we were to blow into the base, which would undoubtedly produce the same result.

### *TO CONSTRUCT THE FIRE-VESSEL CALLED A MILIARIUM, and to make an animal’s mouth blow into the coals, by whose breath the fire burns; and also to ensure that the hot water does not come out unless cold water is first placed in the miliarium, which, because it does not mix quickly with the hot water, will not express water until the cold water reaches the bottom. And to ensure that the water expressed is very cold. Theorem LXXIII.*

# ON PNEUMATICS

...and near the bottom of it, let there be a perforated tube, specifically one of those located beneath the embers; one part of this tube should be closed so that the water from the <term>miliarium|A classical vessel used for heating water, often featuring internal chambers for coals, similar to a modern samovar</term> does not enter it. The other two tubes should reach the area where the water is kept, so that the lit embers or coals in the small space may produce vapors. These vapors pass through a perforated tube in the lid of the miliarium and through the body of the animal figure until they reach its mouth. This animal, looking downwards, will constantly blow <note>The steam acts as an automated bellows, fanning the coals to keep the fire hot.</note> due to the vapor created by the fire. If we wish the vapor to be more powerful, we shall place a little water in the small space enclosed by the two partitions; thus, as the animal blows more strongly, the miliarium heats up even further, and the vapor will rise exactly as we see steam rise from boiling water. The animal's <term>pivot-plug|original: "police," likely meaning a thumb-sized plug or joint</term> should be fitted into a tube in such a way that by lifting it, one can pour a little water through said tube; similarly, when we no longer wish the animal to blow, we can turn its pivot-plug to another side. There should also be a small vessel placed on the lid of the miliarium, from which a pipe extends down near the base of the miliarium, so that cold water can be sent to the bottom through it. But so that the miliarium may be filled by the water poured into the small vessel, and so that the boiling hot water does not splash out, let another perforated tube be fixed to the lid of the miliarium, through which the rising water may fall back into the hollow of the small vessel placed above the lid, as will be seen from the diagram below; and the method of making it shall be this:

Let a hollow cylinder be made, the lower part of which is **A.B.** and the upper part **C.D.** Also make another cylinder smaller than the first, but arranged inside the larger one on the same axis; its lower part shall be **E.F.** and the upper part **G.H.** These upper and lower parts should be closed with two <term>diaphragms|internal partitions or plates used to seal chambers</term> so that air cannot enter in any way. But within the cylinder **E.F.G.H.** let there be tubes **K.O.**, **L.X.**, and **M.N.**, all of which should be perforated inside except for tube **L.X.**, of which only one part should be perforated, namely at **X**. The mouths of these **K** tubes must terminate in the space contained between the two cylinders. This space is divided by two partitions; and into one of these parts, which let us call **E.G.F.H.**, the mouth **X** of the tube **L.X.** (which I said should be half-perforated) shall penetrate. In this same space, there should be a tube **Z.Y.** that reaches the level of the surface of the cylinder lid and is perforated there. Into this, another tube is fixed, the upper mouth of which is formed into an animal figure. This animal is hollowed out by the said tube, and let its mouth be turned toward the coal vessel. The animal should be arranged so that it can be turned by the tube **Y.Z.**, so that when we no longer wish it to blow into the fire, we can do so by turning it elsewhere. When we wish to put water into the closed part **E.G.F.H.**, it will be very convenient to pour it through tube **Y.Z.** by removing the animal and then returning it to its place. When the cold water in the aforementioned space is increased, the quantity of vapor that rises will also be much greater and will exit through the animal's mouth. After this, place a basin **R.S.** above the lid **C.D.**, which is perforated with...

# BY HERO.

...it being covered, and from its base let a pipe originate which enters the space between the two cylinders, staying a small distance from the base of the outer cylinder—or at least far enough to allow for the sufficient flow of water. Whenever we wish for a quantity of water to be discharged, we must pour an equal amount of cold water into vessel R. S. This water, descending through the pipe, will enter the reservoir of hot water; the hot water will then rise up through the neck above the lid. This happens because when cold water enters the hot, they do not mix immediately <note>Hero is describing the principle of thermal stratification and displacement: the denser cold water sinks to the bottom, pushing the lighter hot water out through the top spout.</note>. Thus, as often as we like, we shall have as much hot water as the cold water we put in. However, so that we may notice when the water rises, let a small spout <term>hiatulo|from the Latin 'hiatulus,' a small opening or orifice</term> be placed, ending in a small neck above the lid. Indeed, let the lid be pierced and this neck be very well soldered to it, and let this neck face over the vessel R. S. so that as the hot water rises, it falls into vessel R. S. In such a manner is the <term>miliarium|A specialized water-heating vessel, often featuring internal chambers for coals, used in antiquity for baths or domestic needs</term> constructed.

<image-desc>A woodcut technical diagram illustrating a "miliario" (a type of water-heating apparatus). The diagram shows a large cylindrical vessel in a cutaway view. But if it does not seem right to occupy so much space in this way, let the area of the concavity of one cylinder and the curve of the other be placed closer together by the partitions. In this small space, let the animal figure be placed so that from the aforementioned small space, vapor <note>In this context, 'vapor' refers to the steam generated by the heating water.</note> may ascend through the animal [marked] K; through its tube, water may likewise be introduced to cause a greater amount of vapor to rise.

Having constructed the same *miliarium* <term>miliarium|A vessel used for heating water, here repurposed as a boiler</term> with the aforementioned tubes arranged in the manner described previously, and having drilled them as stated, let the tube V. T.—which we shall call the female <note>In engineering, "female" refers to the hollow part of a joint that receives another piece.</note>—be placed upright upon the base. Inside its body, let there be another tube called the male <note>The "male" part is the solid or inner piece that fits into the female sleeve; here it acts as a rotating valve.</note>, labeled K. L., fitted so precisely that no air may enter between them. This male piece should be drilled through from one side to the other with three holes, M. N. X.; similarly, the female tube V. T. should have three other holes that correspond to those in the male, M. N. X. At hole X, place a bent tube, as shown in the figure, which passes through the lid of the *miliarium* and is securely soldered so that the air cannot escape anywhere except through the tube. At the top of this tube, a blowing animal <note>original: "soffiante animale"; likely a dragon or decorative beast as seen in the diagram on the previous page.</note> should be placed, as was mentioned in the previous section.

Then, align the other corresponding holes M and N with two other bent tubes inside the *miliarium*, such as N. P. and M. O. These must also pass through the lid of the *miliarium* and be perfectly soldered (as was said of the other). At the top of these tubes—that is, the parts extending above the lid—one should feature a bird. The interior of this bird must be hollow so it can be filled with water. The tube should be bent and arranged inside the body of the bird so that it chirps or produces a voice created by the breath of the wind; to achieve this, the bend of the tube must reach the water, which, as has been said elsewhere, will produce the song of a bird.

On the other tube—the part extending outside the lid—place the figure of a Triton <note>original: "Titone (Dio Marino)"; while the text says "Titone" (Tithonus), the description of a sea god with a trumpet clearly refers to Triton.</note> (a Sea God) holding a trumpet in his mouth. This tube should be fitted with a reed <term>lingula|A small tongue or reed used in wind instruments to create vibration</term> and a resonator <note>original: "dodoneo"; likely referring to a sounding vessel or a specific type of fitting named after the cauldrons of Dodona.</note> as is customary. When the steam passes through the reed, it will make the trumpet sound. We shall see from experience that when holes M. O. align with their tube, N. P. with the other, and the animal’s tube with X—which we will know by various marks made on the handle K. L.—we can, at our will, make the animal blow, the bird sing, or the trumpet sound. As for the vessel K. S. and the method for making the hot water rise, let it be done as we described in the preceding section.

# BY HERO:

### L 2

...arranged <note>original: "accommodato"</note> in such a way that in pulling the piston <term>embolo|A plunger or piston that moves within a cylinder to displace air or water</term>, or male part, from below, it opens and the air enters the cylinder; and in pushing it upward, it closes; as was described in the tenth theorem of this book. In addition to this, in the upper part of the concave hemisphere E. F. G. H., a hole is made where another tube F. V. is placed, which is perforated along with another transverse tube V. Z. In this transverse tube are placed the ends of the perforated organ pipes <note>original: "trombe," literally trumpets, but here referring to the ranks of pipes</note> at whose open mouths are placed shutters <term>afſario|A sliding valve or shutter that regulates airflow to the pipe</term> with corresponding holes, which, when pulled, close the mouths of the pipes <term>tibie|The Latin term for flutes or reed-pipes, commonly used for organ pipes</term>. Now, if by raising and lowering the rod Y. 3., the piston R. S. ascends, the air entering through the valve <gloss>cartella</gloss> into the empty cylinder will be driven out, closing the hole in the empty cylinder with the aforementioned valve; thus the air will descend through the tube M. L. into the inverted basin, and from there entering into the transverse tube V. Z. via the tube F. V., and from the transverse tube into the pipes or trumpets. This occurs when the holes of the shutters correspond to the mouths of the pipes; sometimes one, sometimes another, and sometimes all of them will yield their desired sound. 

But regarding how they are made to sound—now one, now another, now all together—and how they are made to fall silent, I shall explain; and let what I say of one pipe be understood of them all. Let a valve or shutter <gloss>cartella</gloss> be made under the mouth of a pipe 1. 2., the mouth of which is 2., and the mouth of the perforated pipe 6., the cover 3. 5., and the hole 5. outside the hole of the pipe. After this, let an elbow-lever <term>cubitolo|A small L-shaped lever or crank, named after the 'cubitus' or elbow</term> be made of three small rods 5. 7. 9. 8., one of which 7. 9. 10. is joined with the cover 9., and at 5. 7. it moves upon a pivot. Thus, if we push the extremity of the lever 8. with our hand toward the interior part under the mouth of the pipe, the cover will move and its hole will come to correspond with the mouth of the pipe. 

But wanting the shutter to return to its place by itself when we remove our hand, and to close the mouth of the valve again, let a rod be placed beneath the shutters parallel to the transverse tube V. Z. and at an equal distance, in which shall be fixed, directly across from the shutters, flexible slats <term>spatule|Spring-like strips used to provide tension</term> made of very resilient <note>original: "nerboſiſſime," meaning sinewy or highly elastic</note> horn. One of these is placed at shutter 1. 2., and at its end let a cord <note>original: "nerbo," likely a gut string or sinew</note> be tied at 7.; so that when the cover is pushed in, it pulls the slat, bending it like a bowstring, and when the slat is released, it pulls the cover back to its place. Thus it changes position; and in this manner, with a shutter or valve arranged under every pipe, when it pleases us to make any of the pipes sound, we shall push the lever 8. with a finger, and when we no longer wish them to sound, we shall remove our fingers, and then, as the levers return to their original place, the sound will cease. 

As for the water which I said should be placed in the vessel A. B. C. D., it serves no other purpose than to ensure that the air—which is in excess in the concave basin when it feels the arrival of breath from the agitated pump <term>modiolo|The pump cylinder</term>—lifts the water; thus the water, by pressing down, causes the pipes to give their sound. But the solid piston R. S., when driven upward as has been said, expresses and drives the air into the concave hemisphere, and when pulled downward, it opens the valve, and through the hole the empty cylinder returns to be filled, so that again the air driven from the solid cylinder goes to the mouths of the pipes in the tube Z. V. This demonstrates that it is well to move the rod T. Y. around the pivot Y., and upon the other is the rod <unclear>ψ</unclear>. <unclear>ψ</unclear>., finding a way to fix it after it has driven the air upward, so that it does not return back when forced.

<vocab>Hero, piston, cylinder, hemisphere, trumpets, pipes, rod/lever, valve/shutter, elbow-lever, spring-slat, pump-bucket, pneumatics</vocab>

It represents one of the earliest descriptions of a keyboard-like interface for a musical instrument.</summary>

## CONSTRUCTING AN ORGAN WHOSE PIPES
### *sound when the wind blows.* Theorem LXXVI.

<image-desc>A technical woodcut illustration of a wind-powered organ mechanism. On the left, a six-vaned vertical windmill (numbered 1 through 6) is mounted on an axle with a crank (Y) and linkage (P, N). This linkage is connected to a piston rod (H) that goes into a tall vertical cylinder (labeled G, R, I). A valve or internal component is labeled T near the bottom, with the base of the cylinder labeled K, N. A horizontal pipe (E, F) connects the base of the cylinder to the lower windchest of the organ. The organ itself is depicted in an architectural frame with a curved pediment and flanking columns. It contains a row of pipes labeled 'A'. The base of the organ is labeled B, D, and C.</image-desc>

Let the trumpets <note>original: "trombe" — here referring to the pipes of the organ</note>, or pipes of the organ, be A; beneath which passes a tube B. C., into which another tube is fixed perpendicularly at D. This perpendicular tube originates from another, such as E. F., which enters into the hollow body of the interior of the cylinder K. L. Within the inner part of this cylinder is placed the valve <term>aſſario|A flap valve or shutter designed to regulate the flow of air, ensuring it moves in only one direction</term> T, which opens and closes freely; and when it is closed, let the seal be made with such diligence <note>original: "diligenza" — implying precision or careful craftsmanship</note> that the air <note>original: "fiato," literally breath or wind</note> cannot escape.

<meta>This page concludes the description of the wind-powered organ (Theorem 76) begun on page 84. It details the mechanical linkage between the windmill sails and the piston pump that provides air to the pipes, concluding the entire treatise on pneumatics.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>OF HERON.</header>

And around the said cylinder, let there be fitted two rings that rotate very easily, such as G and K. These should have two brackets projecting out from it, in which a rod R is fixed. Upon this rod, the windmill-wheel <note>original: "ruota-volatile" — literally a "flying wheel," here meaning the sails of a windmill</note> is mounted, similar to those of windmills, whose sails <note>original: "palle," literally 'balls,' but referring to the curved paddles or vanes seen in the illustration</note> are marked 4, 5, 2, 6, 7. On the axle of this wheel, let there be a crank <term>manico inzanchato|A "shanked" or cranked handle, an early mechanical description of a crankshaft</term> Y, X, 3, like those used for whetstones for sharpening knives and weapons. 

After this, let a cylinder be made with a lathe, which fits perfectly into the hollow tube or cylinder K. L. This must be fitted with such excellence that no air can escape between the surface of the hollow cylinder and that of the solid one <note>the piston</note>. In the middle of its upper part, let there be a rod H. N. fixed into it, which has a hole through which the cranked handle passes. This handle will raise the solid cylinder through the hollow cylinder; then, as the wheel depresses the solid cylinder, air enters through the valve <term>aſſario|A flap valve designed to let air in but not out</term> T. As this valve closes, for the reasons argued elsewhere in this work, it follows that the air, being forced through the tubes E. F. D. B. C., will cause the trumpets <note>original: "trombe" — referring to the organ pipes</note> to sound, which is what we set out to demonstrate above.

# HOW TO MAKE A DRAGON,
## which stands guard over the golden apples, fight a Hercules armed with a club; and while he raises it, the Dragon shall hiss, and at the moment Hercules strikes it on the head, make it so that it sprays water in his face. Theorem I.

---

<image-desc>Horizontal decorative woodcut divider featuring symmetrical floral scrolls and stylized foliage.</image-desc>

Let the base be A.B.C.D., of which one part, C.D. E.F.G.H. K., is most excellently sealed, so that no air can escape from it. Above this, under the channel S, let the funnel <note>original: "infũdibulo"</note> T be placed; the tail of which is narrowed toward the end, as shown by the part marked V. It should stand above the bottom of the base G. H. K. as far as is necessary for the flow of water. Into this bottom, let two bent siphons X and Y be soldered; but let Y be much thinner than X. Then, let Hercules be placed where P is, the golden apple where N is, and below it, where L is, let the Dragon be placed. This being done, place the pipe M in the side of the base E. F. G. H., which turns at O and reaches the mouth of the Dragon in such a fitting manner that it emits a hiss while the air—driven out by the water from channel S entering the base through the funnel—is forced (having no other exit) to leave through said pipe. It should also be arranged in such a way that while the base is being emptied through siphon Y, since it cannot receive air from anywhere else but the mouth of said pipe, it also emits a louder hiss; in my opinion, it is not difficult for anyone to do this. After this, let the lever <term>regolo|A balance beam or straight bar used in mechanics</term> DD. CC. be supported by the pivot OO. Under one end, CC., let the hollow cone <note>original: "conno" — likely a conical valve or plunger housing</note> RR. be fitted. The inside should be worked with a lathe to perfect circles and very straight lines. Inside this, let the solid cone BB. be fitted so that it fits perfectly. This solid cone should have a ring on its upper part, to which a rope is tied and attached at CC., with the lever DD. CC. remaining perfectly level. Under DD., let the vessel Z be hung, which (when empty) is much lighter than the solid cone BB. This vessel, the lever, and the cone should be arranged in such a way that vessel Z stands under the exterior leg of the bent siphon X, and to its handle is tied—

through the bent siphon X, and the water will fall into vessel Z. This vessel, which by its own weight must descend, will cause Hercules to raise his club and will lift the <term>cone<gloss>original: "conno"</gloss></term> BB. In the meantime, water descending through the bent siphon X into vessel AA will enter the empty cone RR. It will happen that once the base A.B.E.F.G.H.I.K. is emptied, vessel Z will also tip over, because the angle of its bottom is so acute that it cannot remain upright. Thus lightened, it will be pulled up by the solid cone BB; and immediately, as the club Q descends, it will strike the Dragon on the head. At the moment of this blow, the Dragon will spray water in Hercules' face; for since the funnel T is positioned almost level with the Dragon's mouth, and the siphon X provides water to vessel AA (from which the pipe TT.R. proceeds into cone RR), this cone will fill up, giving way to the solid piece B as vessel Z descends. The pipe QQ will also fill up near the Dragon's mouth. As the cone BB descends with force, the water inside the hollow space RR—unable to return upward because the path is closed by the <term>device<gloss>original: "affario"</gloss></term> mentioned above—will be forced to escape through pipe QQ to the Dragon's mouth. The Dragon will then spray it (at the same moment the club strikes him) into Hercules' face by the force of weight BB. However, to ensure the water does not escape out of the mouth of the hollow cone RR, but is instead forced into pipe QQ, a leather cone should be made inside the surface of the hollow RR, very well nailed to its mouth. The tip of this leather cone should also be nailed to the tip of the solid cone BB; thus, when the solid cone rises, the leather one will follow it, creating the space described above for the water. This is what was promised in this proposition.

## *HOW TO MAKE SIX RIVERS, MORE OR LESS, POUR WATER FROM THEIR SKINS INTO A LARGE VESSEL; AND IN THIS WATER SHALL BE HIDDEN A TRITON WHO EMERGES QUICKLY FROM THE WAVES AND SOUNDS A TRUMPET OR A CONCH; AND WHILE HE SOUNDS, THE RIVERS SHALL CEASE POURING WATER; AND AS HE DIVES BACK INTO THE WATER, THEY SHALL ONCE AGAIN POUR WATER FROM THE SKINS INTO THE VESSEL, AS IF HE COMMANDED THEM TO STOP RUNNING, AND THEY OBEYED WHILE HE REMAINED ABOVE THE WATER, BUT ONCE HE HAS DEPARTED, THEY NO LONGER HEED THE COMMAND GIVEN TO THEM. THEOR. II.*

Let the base A.B.C.D.E. be very well sealed on all sides. Above it, let there be a wide and capacious vessel F.G., which can be much larger or smaller than the base according to the requirements. Around this vessel, place statues of the rivers I.K.L.M. made of bronze or copper. These should rest on the rim of the vessel, within which is the channel QQ. Their feet should be placed upon this channel and very well soldered to it. For each foot of the river statues, there should be at least one hole through which water can enter the statues. They should be arranged in such a way that from the <term>skins<gloss>original: "vtri" - traditional water skins held by river gods</gloss></term> (which they will carry on their shoulders or under their feet as we please), they pour water into the vessel F.G. This occurs when water falls from channel O. into vessel P., then descends through channel R. into QQ, where the solid piece S. is made, through which the channel passes, and said solid piece

...fitted better; so that the breath <note>In this context, "breath" refers to compressed air.</note> cannot escape, as was described in <term>Theorem IX of Hero<gloss>Hero of Alexandria, an ancient Greek mathematician and engineer known for his pneumatic inventions.</gloss></term> regarding the concave sphere, and in Theorem XXVII regarding the pipes used for firefighting. Let the <term>slider<gloss>original: "regolo"; a moving part of a valve.</gloss></term> be made from the tube at *Y*, pierced through the middle; and once fixed at *Y*, let the Triton stand there. Through the body of this Triton, let there be a hollow pipe soldered to the hole of the slider, and let it reach the mouth of this Triton and enter the conch shell. Inside the shell, a <term>reed<gloss>original: "lingula"; a small vibrating strip that produces sound, much like in a modern woodwind instrument.</gloss></term> should be fitted, just as <term>peasants<gloss>original: "Villani"</gloss></term> are accustomed to fitting them in their <term>bagpipes<gloss>original: "Piue"</gloss></term>.

Afterwards, at *Y*, let vessel 7 be hung with a rope, and inside it place a pneumatic tube. T.*, which is <term>pivoted<gloss>original: "bilicata"</gloss></term> at 16. To one end of the rope at pulley 2, hang weight 6; to the other end of the rope—that is, the one over pulley 4—tie the handle of vessel 7, which should be light enough to be easily pulled upward by weight 6.

Then, inside vessel *F. G.*, fit the pneumatic tube 9, which pours water into cup 10, from which channel 10–11 originates. In this same vessel, also place the <term>bent siphon<gloss>original: "siffone inflessa"</gloss></term> or pneumatic tube 14; one leg of which enters the base *A. B. C. D. E.*, and the other should stay as far above the bottom of the vessel as we judge appropriate for the flow of water. Do the same for tube 9. In the base, place the bent siphon 15 and, if needed, another, or the pneumatic tube 17.

Once this is completed, it will be seen that as water flows through the channel *R. S. Q. Q.*, because vessel *P* is positioned high, the water will rise to the <term>water-skins<gloss>original: "vtri"</gloss></term> held on the shoulders of the <term>river gods<gloss>original: "fiumi"</gloss></term>. They will then pour water into vessel *F. G.*, which will fill up according to the principles established by Hero in his first and second Theorems. The water will then descend through the bent siphon 14 into the base *A. B. C. D. E.*

The air that is within the base will be seen to compress as the water—another body—enters and takes its place. Because the river gods pour a greater volume of water, the vessel will rise, and the pneumatic tube 9 will also pour into cup 10. The water, flowing through channel 10–11, will fall into vessel 5. When this vessel is full, its weight will cause it to descend, and at the same time, the rod *T. V.* will turn, closing the channel in the solid block 5.

Consequently, the river gods will stop pouring water. As the end of the slider 11 lowers—because it can pivot on the pierced tube—the Triton will emerge from the water. The hole of pipe *X* will then align with the hole of the tube, and the compressed air in the base, finding an open exit, will burst out with force. This will cause the conch shell in the Triton’s mouth to sound. 

When the base is filled with water, the bent siphon 15 and the pneumatic tube 17 will empty it, and the base will fill with air once again through the hole in the Triton's conch shell. Meanwhile, as the pneumatic tube 13 empties vessel 5, weight 6 will pull the now-empty vessel upward. This opens the water channel to the river gods again, and the Triton, by its own weight, will dive back into the water. These cycles will continue to be observed as long as the channel *O* flows, which is exactly what was proposed.

# HOW TO MAKE A FORGE APPEAR TO GLOW WITH THE WATER OF A SINGLE CHANNEL,

in which a <term>Blacksmith<gloss>original: "Fabro"</gloss></term> holds an iron to heat, then turns and places it on the anvil, and immediately three other blacksmiths strike the said iron in sequence <note>original: "in terzo"; referring to a rhythmic, alternating striking pattern common in smithing.</note>, and every blow causes water to splash out, just as sparks fly from red-hot iron when struck. Theorem III.

as for the flow of the water, it will seem to us that it is sufficient, and from the rope of the <term>pulley<gloss>original: "troclea"</gloss></term> 27, let the weight 19 be hung. This weight should be just heavy enough to have the strength to turn the pipe and pull the vessel 20 along with it. After this, the basin 21 should be placed under the center of the <term>spindle<gloss>original: "fuſo"</gloss></term>; this basin should have the channel 22, 23, the mouth of which sits above vessel 20. We shall see that as water runs through channel **H.I.**, it will turn the wheel 5, 6, 7, 8, because as the water falls from mouth **I** into the hollows of the wheel 9, 10, 11, 12, it must necessarily turn due to the weight of the water. As it turns, the iron rods **C. C.** will go along striking the iron pins 3, which, turning upon the centers of the rollers, will lower the head 4. Thus, the ropes that pass through the legs of the <term>Blacksmiths<gloss>original: "Fabri"</gloss></term> will be pulled, causing them to raise their arms. Their hammers will <term>strike in thirds<gloss>original: "batteranno in terzo"</gloss></term> <note>This refers to a rhythmic, alternating pattern of striking typical of multiple smiths working one anvil.</note> upon the anvil. Because the <term>cross-piece<gloss>original: "crociera"</gloss></term> of the wheel will be empty (although it is

<image-desc>A circular diagram representing a water wheel or turbine. It features multiple concentric rings with several curved, blade-like vanes radiating from the center to the outer circumference, designed to catch water flow. necessary that these holes be small, so that only a little water passes through them), the water will descend into the center of the spindle, and from there it will flow into basin 21, and from that into vessel 20 through channel 22, 23. When this vessel is full, it will descend downward due to its weight, pulling weight 19 with it and turning the pipe 13, 14 upon the pivot fixed at 15. Consequently, as Blacksmith **E** turns, it will appear that he is carrying the iron to heat in the <term>Forge<gloss>original: "Fucina"</gloss></term>. This must be adjusted precisely so that as he turns, he places the iron into the "boiling" of the water, which will truly appear to boil. This happens because as the pipe 13, 14 turns, channel **H.I.** will close, and since the wheel will no longer turn, the Blacksmiths must stop. But because the hole of the pipe will then turn into channel **L**, the water will rise to basin **N** through channel **L. O. M.** and will boil—remembering to arrange it so that the boiling water does not pass a certain limit by making holes through which it may drain away. Meanwhile, vessel 20 will empty itself through its <term>diabetes<gloss>original: "diabete"</gloss></term> <note>In ancient and Renaissance mechanics, a "diabetes" refers to a specific type of siphon used to regulate fluid levels.</note> or siphon, which are one and the same, and the weight 19 will again return to raise vessel 20. By turning the pipe 13 and 14, Blacksmith **E** will return to place the iron on the anvil as channel **C** opens once more. As this again causes the wheel to turn, the Blacksmiths will work anew. As they strike upon cone **P**—that is, upon the solid part—because the void will remain almost full of water due to the small channel **Q. R.**, every strike of the hammer will cause water to splash out. This is because the surface of the solid part will not touch the surface of the void, being supported by <term>springs<gloss>original: "ſuſte"</gloss></term> as we have said, which is our intended purpose.

# TO CONSTRUCT A ROOM IN WHICH
### at any time we please, a wind will always blow to cool it, a little or a lot as we wish. Theorem IV.

<image-desc>A detailed technical woodcut illustration showing a cross-section of a building. The top part shows a room labeled A and B with a tiled floor and an ornate coffered ceiling. Five human-like figures or statues, numbered 1 through 5, stand against the back wall. Below the floor is a mechanical substructure divided into two main chambers labeled X and Y. Various siphons and conduits (labeled K, L, M, N, P, R, S, T, U, Z) are depicted, with water pouring from a large siphon at the bottom. To the left, an external lever or pipe system labeled Q is attached to the structure.</image-desc>

Let there be excavated beneath the floor of the room **A.B.C.D.E.** as much as seems sufficient according to the quantity of wind we desire—a chamber as wide as the room itself and at least ten feet in height. Let it be very well plastered and partitioned with <term>lime mortar<gloss>original: "calcina"</gloss></term> mixed with an equal amount of finely crushed stone (more or less, depending on the quality of the lime). It should be divided into two chambers by a vault or partition, labeled **X** and **Y**, each of which will be 5 feet high. Once plastered, for a space of eight days, one must go every day to wet the plaster, smoothing and polishing it perfectly through the work of a <term>mason<gloss>original: "Moratore"</gloss></term> <note>This is an archaic spelling of the Italian "muratore."</note>, in such a way that these chambers hold both air and water so that they cannot escape from any side. In these, fit the two large siphons **S**, **T**, and **s**, so that their long legs enter into the lower chamber while their short legs stand above the pavement of the upper chamber, at a depth sufficient for the flow of the water; and similarly the siphon...

...siphon **T**, of which the lower leg **V** extends below the lowest floor and ends in a channel that carries the water away. In the upper room, let the <term>funnel<gloss>original: "infondibulo"</gloss></term> **P** be made of either natural stone or copper, its tail **R** resting as far above the floor **X. Y.** as seems sufficient for the flow of water. Then, arrange the <term>mouths of the winds<gloss>original: "bocche de i venti"</gloss></term> <note>These are air vents designed to look like decorative faces or apertures that "breathe" out cool air.</note> throughout the room as we desire. Let the channels **1. F. 2. G. 3. N. 4. I. 5. K. 6. L. 7. M. 8. 1.** be made; the lower mouths of these enter the first chamber through the floor of the room, while the others connect to the air vents. As the water rises through the pipe **Q** into the funnel **P** and above the floor **X. Y.**, it will force out a corresponding amount of air through the air vents, making the room cool because those vents will blow like mouths of wind. To ensure they blow continuously, other channels can be made for the vents **1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 6. 7. 8.** that descend through the wall into the lower chamber with open mouths. When the water above the floor **X. Y.** has risen high enough to pass entirely through the siphon **S**, the first chamber will empty itself into the lower chamber; as the water rises above the floor of the lower chamber, it will force more air out through the vents **1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 6. 7. 8.**, and this chamber, in turn,

once filled with water, will empty through the siphon **T**, exiting by way of **V**. Note that siphon **S** must be large enough to overcome the flow from the tail **R** of the vessel **P** when the chamber cycles, and thus one will have a very cool, continuous wind in the proposed room at all times,
