# METHOD FOR RAISING
## A CHANNEL OF WATER,
### running or still, to the top of any high tower.

### ALREADY USED IN MANY PLACES,
->*provided that the waters have a slight drop from their surface.*<-

---

<image-desc>Decorative horizontal woodcut ornament consisting of stylized floral motifs and fleur-de-lis arranged symmetrically.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large decorative drop-capital 'P' set within a square frame, featuring intricate foliate scrollwork and vine patterns.</image-desc>Since the creation of natural fountains in the lowlands <note>Specifically referring to the "Low Countries" or any flat geographical area lacking natural elevation for gravity-fed water.</note> is not permitted by the nature of the landscape, it is necessary to create them through artifice, both in your lands and in any other similar place. To ensure that the curious do not have to give up their desire—due to the difficulty of water flow—to put into practice what was written by the most excellent mathematician Hero <note>Hero of Alexandria (c. 10–70 AD), a Greek engineer and mathematician who described early steam power and hydraulic machines.</note> and the four methods I have demonstrated, I wanted to add to this (in my judgment) most beautiful book the present method of raising a <term>channel of running water<gloss>original: "Canale d'acqua viua"</gloss></term> to any great height. In this way, what nature does not grant on a plain can be obtained through art by a very easy method and with very light expense for those who have nearby rivers, canals, or any other running water. 

The method of doing this is understood almost without writing from the drawing; yet, it does not seem appropriate to me to omit writing about the way to build this beautiful <term>structure<gloss>original: "edificio"</gloss></term>. I am reserving many other ways of raising water for when it pleases God to give me enough leisure that I may finish the fine general rules of <term>Architecture<gloss>original: "Architettura"</gloss></term> I began long ago. Let a <term>wheel<gloss>original: "ruota"</gloss></term> be made, then, the diameter of which is at least five or six feet. It should be as light as possible, made of the very best <term>oak wood<gloss>original: "legno di rouere"</gloss></term> so that it lasts in the water, and its thickness should be at least one and a half feet. From the <term>outer edge<gloss>original: "abfide"</gloss></term> or the extreme line of its greatest diameter toward the center, let a bottom one foot thick be made. Then, let at least fifteen <term>spaces<gloss>original: "spatij"</gloss></term> be divided on the line of the wheel’s circumference, and let the partitions be curved, like a half-C, as figure A.B. clearly shows. The compartments are labeled C, D, E, F. Let it then be constructed with very good cross-braces of good oak wood (a wood that lasts a long time in the water) with its diameters well-fitted...

On the left, an overshot wheel (labeled A and B) is turned by water falling from an upper wooden trough. On the right, a paddle-style wheel (labeled G and H) with individual vanes or buckets (labeled I, K, L, M) is turned by water flowing through a lower stone-lined channel. Both wheels are connected to a single long axle with multiple crank offsets (labeled N, O, R, S, T, V). The difference that does exist is this: the wheel with paddles can be turned with a smaller <term>drop of water<gloss>original: "caduta d'acqua"</gloss></term> <note>This refers to the "head" or the vertical distance the water falls to provide energy.</note>, because if these paddles are made wide enough, the wheel will turn with very little drop and with a small quantity of water, as we see every day in our **mills on the Po** <note>The Po River in Italy was famous for its floating mills, which used undershot wheels designed to work with the river's steady but low-velocity flow.</note>, for example. Once this is done, let the center axle be made of an iron rod bent as many times and as much as is shown, and as much as seems appropriate to us according to the amount of water we wish to raise, or according to the strength of the water’s flow that will turn the wheel. # T H E O R E M S

...better at **P. Q.** This place in the center will serve for pivots to turn the spindle, allowing the wheel to rotate upon two wooden beams, stones, or walls, as may be convenient, provided that beneath these pivots are placed their <term>bearings<gloss>original: "tampagni"</gloss></term> made of bronze. This material is worn away much less by iron, and it wears the iron down much less than iron does against iron, which grinds away in an instant. In the middle of the bends, as at **R. S. T. V. X.**, bronze rings should be placed so they do not "eat" the iron. Inside the heads of these, iron rods with holes should be fixed so they meet, where more than one iron wedge may pass through each ring to join them together, as shown in example **A. A.** These iron rods should be made at least as long as the half-diameter of the wheel.

Below these, positioned perpendicularly, one should place the bronze <term>cylinders<gloss>original: "modioli"</gloss></term> with <term>valves<gloss>original: "assarij"</gloss></term> at the bottom. As Vitruvius, Vegetius, and Valturio <note>Vitruvius (1st c. BC), Vegetius (4th c. AD), and Roberto Valturio (15th c. AD) were the primary historical authorities on Roman and Renaissance engineering.</note> say regarding the <term>Ctesibian Machine<gloss>original: "Machina Chtesibica"</gloss></term>, these are the <term>flaps<gloss>original: "cartelle"</gloss></term> in the <term>pumps<gloss>original: "trombe"</gloss></term> <note>Literally "trumpets," referring to the shape of the pump chambers.</note> used to draw water from ships and every low place. On one side of these, another hole should be bored, and other flaps attached to the cylinders; but these must be jointed and arranged in such a way that when "drawing breath" (suction) through the mouths **2. 3. 4. 5.**, these holes close and the bottom ones open. When "blowing" inside (expulsion), the bottom ones close and these open. The locations for these are **6. 7. 8. 9.**

To each of these cylinders, a pipe should be soldered with a mouth wide enough that the said valves or flaps can play within them; but they should be somewhat narrower at the other end. These then join together into a single pipe, as seen in the figure at number **10.** This pipe, making an angle as at **11.**, will rise perpendicularly as high as we please, as at **12.** Next, to the iron rods—which are called <term>connecting rods<gloss>original: "asticiuole"</gloss></term>—solid leather cylinders (pistons) should be attached. These should be jointed at the connection of the rods, it being necessary to place a medium-sized iron pin through them to hold the leather discs together. These are placed in the cylinders, rising and falling so exactly that they can draw in the air through the valves and drive it out.

By letting the water from channel **13.** or **14.** fall upon the wheel, the wheel will turn; the pistons, moving up and down, will draw the water into the spindle as they rise and drive it out through pipes **6. 7. 8. 9.** into pipes **10.** and **11.** as they descend. The water will be so forced by the power of the turning wheel that it will be pushed upward as high as we wish to send it. We must remember, when it arrives at the intended place, to make a receiving vessel there from which another pipe derives to carry it downward; by its great fall, it will perform whatever we desire. If too much water enters that vessel, another pipe can be installed to allow the excess to drain away by itself.

We should keep in mind that all the cylinders must remain in the water. It might be best to keep the wheel's location separate from that of the cylinders; for any water, even if muddy and turbid, is perfectly good for turning the wheel. However, to spray it with the pistons in the cylinders, the water must be purified (filtered), so that the outlets of the pipes do not become clogged with mud, which would stop the water inside. Provision is usually made for this with <term>strainers<gloss>original: "foradori"</gloss></term>. And to ensure the water does not rise above the pistons but stays always at a certain level, it will be necessary to make overflow outlets so it cannot pass the determined point. For the rest, one can very easily understand the artifice from the drawing itself, as it seems to me that nothing else...

# A D D I T I O N S

For now, I need not say more on this matter, except to demonstrate how this very same result—which we have stated is achieved by running water—can be easily accomplished by a man or by a horse. I shall not fatigue myself further in describing this, as it seems to me that the drawings of these two methods are sufficient in themselves to be understood. As for the cause of the speed of <term>circular motions<gloss>original: "moti circulari"</gloss></term>, we shall speak of that when it pleases God that we may demonstrate how weights are pulled and pushed.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a complex mechanical pumping system powered by manual labor. On the left, a large vat contains three vertical cylinders (pumps) with piston rods connected to a horizontal multi-crankshaft. This shaft has a large vertical gear wheel (marked L) that meshes with a horizontal toothed gear wheel (marked K). A vertical axle (marked E) rises from wheel K and supports a four-armed horizontal flywheel with weights at the ends (marked G, H, I, P). On the right, a man (marked A) operates the machine by pushing a long horizontal lever (marked B) attached to the central axle. The lever is supported by a structural frame with a flexible horizontal spring or guide (marked C) at the top. A set of stairs leads up to the raised platform where the man is working. A partial view of a water wheel is visible at the very bottom.</image-desc>

I will only add that this method of raising and lowering the <term>leather cylinders<gloss>original: "cilindri di cuoio"</gloss></term> <note>These are leather-clad pistons or plungers designed to create a tight seal within the pump.</note> within the bronze <term>pump-chambers<gloss>original: "modioli"</gloss></term> <note>From the Latin "modiolus," referring to the bucket or cylinder of a pump.</note> by the power of a single man—indeed, even a weak boy—has proved many times to be extremely fast (owing to the principles of circular motion demonstrated by Aristotle in the <term>*Mechanics*<gloss>original: "Mecanice"</gloss></term> <note>This refers to the *Mechanical Problems*, a Greek treatise attributed to the school of Aristotle that explains the physics of levers, pulleys, and circular motion.</note>), since the force moving...

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the pump mechanism, focusing on the physics of leverage and momentum provided by a flywheel (the "wooden Cross") and the conversion of circular motion via gears to drive the pistons. ### THEOREMS

...the moving force at A <gloss>original: "mouente in A"</gloss>, because it is far from the center (which is the axis of the handle B), causes the motion. Since the second force at C is less distant from the center B, it is easily moved by the force at A. However, the third force, which is D, must have a radius greater than C and smaller than A. This is because the wooden Cross <note>This "wooden Cross" functions as a flywheel, using centrifugal force and momentum to maintain steady motion once started.</note> placed on the spindle E, with the weights F, G, H, and I hung from it, makes the motion forceful <gloss>original: "violente"</gloss> once they have gained momentum, which significantly reduces the required moving force. By placing the drum <term>timpano<gloss>original: "timpano, ò ruotella dentata"</gloss><note>A large circular gear wheel.</note></term> or toothed wheel K on the spindle E, and ensuring the teeth mesh between the rungs of the <term>lantern gear<gloss>original: "rochella"</gloss><note>A cage-like pinion gear commonly used in early machinery to translate motion between axes.</note></term> L fixed into the crankshaft, the small rods <gloss>original: "hasticiuole"</gloss> attached to the shafts of the <term>leather cylinders<gloss>original: "cilindri di cuoio"</gloss><note>These are leather-clad pistons or plungers that create a seal inside the pump.</note></term> will move. These, through the pump chambers <term>modioli<gloss>original: "modioli"</gloss><note>The cylindrical chambers where the pumping action occurs.</note></term> arranged with their fittings as I described above, will cause the water to flow to any desired height with great speed.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a mechanical water pump powered by a horse. On a raised wooden platform, a horse led by a man is harnessed to a long wooden lever (stanga). This lever rotates a central vertical shaft (fuso) that passes through the platform floor. A circular track on the platform marks the horse's path. Beneath the platform, the shaft is fitted with a large horizontal gear (timpano) that meshes with a vertical cage-like pinion (rochella). This pinion is connected to a horizontal crankshaft. Three vertical rods (hasticiuole) are attached to the cranks and extend downward into three cylinders (modioli) positioned in a body of water at the bottom left. As the horse walks in a circle, the mechanism creates a reciprocating motion that pumps water upward through a vertical pipe/spout.</image-desc>

The same motion with the same speed will be achieved if a long bar <gloss>original: "stanga"</gloss> is fixed to the spindle (the one in which the toothed wheel is fixed, whose teeth go between the rungs of the lantern gear L that turns the center where the cylinder rods hang, moving up and down through the pump chambers). This bar should be long enough so that by attaching a horse to it, the animal can comfortably walk in a circle around the spindle fixed in the ground.

### To construct a vessel into which, when liquid is poured, it will be received; but when no more water is poured, it will receive no more. 42

Upon a base may be placed a <term>Satyr<note>In Greek mythology, a woodland spirit with horse-like or goat-like features, often associated with wine and the god Dionysus.</note></term> holding a <term>wineskin<gloss>original: "Vtre"</gloss></term> in his hands, beneath which there is a <term>basin<gloss>original: "auello"</gloss></term>; if this is filled with water, the water will fall through the wineskin into the said basin; nor will it ever overflow the edges of the vessel until all the water has been evacuated through the wineskin, and the method of constructing it is this. 43

### To construct an altar upon which, once a fire is lit, the doors of a Temple suddenly open, and once the fire is extinguished, they suddenly close again.

*Certain siphons <note>original: "siffoni".</note> placed in some vessels discharge the water until either the vessels* 
*are empty, or until the surface of the water reaches the level of the*
*mouth of the siphons; but (if it shall be necessary) to make it so they no longer*
*flow.* 74
*Once a fire is lit upon an altar, to make certain animals circle around*
*in the manner of dances; but let the altars be transparent, either with glass or*
*very thin, pure bone.* 75
*To construct an ingenious lamp with oil inside, which, when it is*
*lacking, more can be added as much as we please without an external*
*oil vessel.* 76
*To construct the fire-vessel called a <term>Miliario <note>A type of water heater or boiler, named for its resemblance to a Roman milestone.</note></term>, and make an animal's*
*mouth blow into the coals—by which breath the fire burns—and also to make it so*
*that the hot water does not come out unless cold water is first placed in the <unclear>Miliario</unclear>,*
*which, because it does not mix so quickly with the hot water,*
*therefore will not discharge <note>original: "esprimerà"; literally "to squeeze out."</note> water until the cold water*
*reaches the bottom. And to make it so that the coldest water is discharged.* 77
*Miliari are also used, constructed with other craftsmanship, to make*
*trumpets sound and birds sing artificially.* 80
*To assemble the <term>Hydraulic Instrument <note>original: "Instrumento Hidraulico"; referring to the Hydraulis, an ancient water-powered organ.</note></term>.* 81
*To construct an organ of which the trumpets sound when the* [wind blows...]

<summary>This page lists several sophisticated mechanical and hydraulic inventions, including a "treasury" machine for temples, automated dancing figures on altars, and the complex mechanics of a water organ. It highlights the use of siphons and temperature-based water displacement in early modern or classical engineering.</summary>

The devices listed here are increasingly theatrical, involving mythological figures and complex synchronized movements.</meta>

wind. <margin>84</margin>

<margin>88</margin>
To make a Hercules, with a mace, fight with a Dragon that stands on guard of the golden apples <note>A reference to the eleventh labor of Hercules, involving the Garden of the Hesperides.</note>; and while he raises the mace, let the Dragon hiss, and at the moment that Hercules strikes it on the head, make the Dragon spray water into his face.

<margin>90</margin>
To make six rivers—be they more or less—pour water from their skins <note>original: "Vtri"; refers to goatskin bottles or bladders often used in fountain iconography to represent the source of a river.</note> into a large vessel, and in this water let there be a hidden Triton, who quickly emerges from the waves and sounds a Trumpet or Conch shell; and while he sounds it, let the rivers cease pouring water; and as he dives back into the water, make them return again to pouring water from the skins into the vessel, as if he commands them to stop flowing, and they halt while he stands above the water, but once he has departed, they no longer heed the command given to them.

<margin>93</margin>
To make a forge seen to boil <note>In this context, "boil" likely refers to the glowing heat or the bustling activity of the workshop.</note> by the water of a single channel, in which a Blacksmith holds a piece of iron to heat; then let him turn and place it upon the anvil, and immediately three other Blacksmiths strike the said iron in sequence <note>original: "in terzo"; a rhythmic pattern where three smiths strike in alternating succession.</note>, and every blow makes water splash out, just as sparks fly from the red-hot beaten iron.

<margin>96</margin>
To construct a room in which, at the time that pleases us, a wind always blows to refresh it, and very much to our liking.

<margin>98</margin>
A way to make a channel of water rise by the force of water <note>A reference to hydraulic pressure or a "water ram" effect to move water upward.</note> to the top of any high Tower.
